<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{ipm:main}
There are an odd mean-zero $\rho_{\rm in}\in C^\infty(\mathbb T^2)$,
a time $T>0$, and a solution of \eqref{ipm:unforced} in
$C^\infty([0,T)\times\mathbb T^2)$, with $\rho(0)=\rho_{\rm in}$,
such that
\[
 \lim_{t\uparrow T}\partial_2\rho(t,0)=+\infty,
 \qquad
 \lim_{t\uparrow T}\partial_1u_1(\rho)(t,0)=+\infty.
\]
There is a common terminal density $\rho_*$ such that
$\rho(t)\to\rho_*$ in $C^\eta$ for every $0\le\eta<1$.
By replacing $\rho(t,x)$ with $T\rho(Tt,x)$, one can arrange $T=1$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{ipm:amplification}
Fix $c>0$. There are constants $x_0$, $j_0$, and $\epsilon_0>0$
with the following property. Suppose that, on $[s,s+2W]$, a parent has
the following decompositions at the origin:
\begin{align}
 r_P&=G n+R_r,&
 M_P&=-cG\cos\alpha\,n^\perp\otimes n+R_M,
       \label{ipm:transfer-decomposition}\\
 H_P&=-cG\sin\alpha\,n\otimes n+R_H,&
 n&=(\cos\alpha,\sin\alpha),
       \nonumber
\end{align}
where $G(s)=H_0\ge1$, $\alpha(s)=\pi/2+1/x$, and
\begin{equation}
 |R_r|+\|R_M\|+\|R_H\|
       +|\alpha'|+|(\log G)'|\le E.
 \label{ipm:transfer-error}
\end{equation}
Let
\begin{equation}
 W=\frac{2x^2}{cH_0},\qquad
 \eta=\frac1{j^2x},\qquad
 j\ge j_0,\quad x\ge x_0,
 \qquad \frac{E j^2x^3}{H_0}\le\epsilon_0.
 \label{ipm:transfer-smallness}
\end{equation}
Choose a child phase with initial angle
$\beta(s)=\alpha(s)-\pi/2+\tau$, where
$|\tau|j^2x\le\epsilon_0$. Its first crossing
$\beta(t_*)=\pi/2+\eta$ occurs with
\begin{equation}
 s+W/4\le t_*\le s+3W/4.
 \label{ipm:transfer-time}
\end{equation}
The phase angle at the origin satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:transfer-angle-range}
 \beta(t)\in[1/(2x),\pi-1/(2x)],\qquad s\le t\le t_*.
\end{equation}
The child amplitude satisfies
\begin{equation}
 e^{c_0x}\le
 \frac{a(t_*,0)|m(t_*,0)|}{a(s,0)|m(s,0)|}
 \le e^{C_0x}
 \label{ipm:transfer-gain}
\end{equation}
for absolute constants $c_0,C_0>0$.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
We give a stopping argument in which no target crossing is assumed.
Throughout this proof constants may depend on the fixed number $c$.
Set
\[
 \mu=\frac{Ex^3}{H_0}\le\epsilon_0j^{-2}.
\]
Since
$2W=4x^2/(cH_0)$, integration of the two derivative bounds yields, for
$s\le t\le s+2W$,
\begin{equation}
 |\alpha(t)-(\pi/2+x^{-1})|+|\log(G(t)/H_0)|
       \le2EW=\frac{4\mu}{cx}\le\frac{4\epsilon_0}{c}\eta.
 \label{ipm:expanded-cascade-parent-drift}
\end{equation}
Fix $\epsilon_0$ so small that this is at most $\eta/100$.
By increasing $x_0,j_0$, we also arrange
that $G/H_0\in[9/10,11/10]$ and
$\alpha-\pi/2\in[9/(10x),11/(10x)]$.

Let $N=(\cos\beta,\sin\beta)$ be the child unit phase. Its equation is
\[
 \beta'=(N^\perp)^T(-M_P^T)N.
\]
For $n=(\cos\alpha,\sin\alpha)$,
$n^\perp\cdot N=\sin(\beta-\alpha)$ and
$N^\perp\cdot n=-\sin(\beta-\alpha)$. Set $q=-cG\cos\alpha$,
$d=\beta-\alpha$, and $z=-\cot d$ whenever $d\in(-\pi,0)$.
Substituting the rank-one part of $M_P$ then gives
\begin{equation}
 \beta'=q(t)\sin^2d+O(E),\qquad
 d'=q(t)\sin^2d+O(E),\qquad
 z'=q(t)+O(E(1+z^2)),
 \label{ipm:transfer-z}
\end{equation}
The identity for $d'$
follows from $d'=\beta'-\alpha'$, the identity for $\beta'$, and
$|\alpha'|\le E$ from \eqref{ipm:transfer-error}.
The identity for $z'$ follows from
$z'=(\sin d)^{-2}d'$ and
$(\sin d)^{-2}=1+z^2$.

Since $-\cos\alpha=\sin(\alpha-\pi/2)
=x^{-1}[1+O(x^{-2}+\mu)]$, \eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-parent-drift} gives
\begin{equation}
 q(t)=\frac{cH_0}{x}
       [1+O(x^{-2}+\mu)].
 \label{ipm:transfer-q}
\end{equation}

Initially $d(s)=-\pi/2+\tau$ and $z(s)=\tan\tau$.
Consider the connected interval beginning at $s$ on which $d\in(-\pi,0)$
and $|z|<2x$. On this interval the last equation in
\eqref{ipm:transfer-z} has error at most $CEx^2$. Relative to
$cH_0/x$ this is at most $C\mu$. Hence
\begin{equation}
 z'=\frac{cH_0}{x}
       [1+O(x^{-2}+\mu)]>0.
 \label{ipm:transfer-monotone}
\end{equation}
We may make the relative error here at most $1/20$. The same choices
give $|z(s)|=|\tan\tau|\le2|\tau|\le2\epsilon_0/(j^2x)\le x/100$.
A first exit before $s+3W/4$ is impossible:
monotonicity excludes the boundary $z=-2x$, while integration gives
\[
 z(t)\le x/100+(21/20)(cH_0/x)(3W/4)
             <2x
\]
for $t\le s+3W/4$. The angle cannot leave $(-\pi,0)$ while $z$
remains bounded, because the two endpoints correspond to poles of
$-\cot d$. Thus all the preceding estimates hold on the complete
interval $[s,s+3W/4]$, whether or not a target has yet been crossed.
\label{ipm:expanded-cascade-stopping}

The target is the moving value
\begin{equation}
 z_{\rm tar}(t)=
    \cot\bigl(\alpha(t)-\pi/2-\eta\bigr).
 \label{ipm:expanded-cascade-moving-target}
\end{equation}
Its argument is positive, since
$\alpha-\pi/2-\eta=x^{-1}(1-j^{-2})+O(\mu/x)$.
The Taylor estimate $\cot u=u^{-1}+O(u)$ for small positive $u$
therefore gives, uniformly in the complete stopped interval,
\[
 z_{\rm tar}(t)=x\,[1+O(j^{-2}+x^{-2}+\mu)].
\]
Choose $j_0,x_0,\epsilon_0$ so that it lies between $0.9x$ and
$1.1x$. On $[s,s+W/4]$, integration of
\eqref{ipm:transfer-monotone} gives $z(t)\le0.54x$, whereas at
$s+3W/4$ it gives $z\ge1.41x$. Continuity forces the first intersection
with \eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-moving-target} between $s+W/4$ and
$s+3W/4$, proving \eqref{ipm:transfer-time}.

Since $z$ is increasing and $d\in(-\pi,0)$, the angle $d$ is
increasing, so $d(t)\ge d(s)=-\pi/2+\tau$. Together with
$9/(10x)\le\alpha-\pi/2\le11/(10x)$, this gives
\[
 \beta=\alpha+d\ge\frac{9}{10x}-|\tau|\ge\frac1{2x},
 \qquad \beta<\alpha\le\frac\pi2+\frac{11}{10x}\le\pi-\frac1{2x}.
\]
Here we use the smallness of $|\tau|$ and sufficiently large $x_0$.
This proves the asserted angle range on $[s,t_*]$.

By \eqref{ipm:rayidentity} and the Hessian decomposition,
\begin{equation}
 (\log(a|m|))'
     =cG\sin\alpha\,\sin^2d+O(E).
 \label{ipm:transfer-log-g}
\end{equation}
To estimate this growth, use $|z|\le2x$ and \eqref{ipm:transfer-q}:
\begin{equation}
 \sin^2d=\frac1{1+z^2}\ge\frac1{1+4x^2},\qquad
 q\sin^2d\ge c_1H_0/x^3.
 \label{ipm:expanded-cascade-angle-denominator}
\end{equation}
Write
$B=q\sin^2d$ and $D=cG\sin\alpha\sin^2d$. Then
$D/B=-\tan\alpha$, $B\ge c_1H_0/x^3$, and the quotient of either
additive error $O(E)$ by $B$ is at most $C\mu$. Therefore
\[
 \frac{(\log(a|m|))'}{d'}
 =-\tan\alpha(t)
       [1+O(\mu)]
       +O(\mu),
\]
where the denominator factor $(1+O(\mu))^{-1}$ was expanded only
after ensuring $|O(\mu)|\le1/4$. The number $-\tan\alpha(t)$ lies
between fixed positive multiples of $x$ by
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-parent-drift}. Moreover,
\[
 d(t_*)-d(s)
 =\pi/2-\tau-[\alpha(t_*)-\pi/2-\eta]
\]
lies, for the fixed choices above, between $1$ and $2$. Integration
with respect to the strictly increasing variable $d$ proves
\eqref{ipm:transfer-gain} with fixed $c_0,C_0>0$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{ipm:profilelemma}
There is a family of odd $2\pi$-periodic Gevrey-two functions $f_\delta$,
$0<\delta\le\delta_0$, with fixed constants $C_f\ge1$ and $\delta_0>0$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:profile-bounds}
 f_\delta'(0)=\delta^{-1},\qquad f_\delta'\ge-C_f,
 \qquad \|f_\delta\|_\infty\le C_f,
 \qquad
 \|f_\delta^{(n)}\|_\infty\le C_f^{n+1}\delta^{-n}(n!)^2.
\end{equation}
There is also a fixed nonnegative even Gevrey-two cutoff $\chi$ supported
in $B_{1/2}(0)$ and equal to one on $B_{1/4}(0)$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Choose the even bump
\[
 \psi(z)=\begin{cases}\exp(1-(1-z^2)^{-1}),&|z|<1,\\0,&|z|\ge1.
 \end{cases}
\]
It satisfies $\psi(0)=1$ and $\|\psi^{(n)}\|_\infty\le C^{n+1}(n!)^2$.
To wit, at a point with $t=1-z^2>0$, a complex disk of radius
$c_0t$ keeps $1-w^2$ in a fixed right sector of size comparable to $t$.
Cauchy's estimate gives
$|\psi^{(n)}(z)|\le n!(C/t)^n e^{-c_1/t}$.
Taking the supremum in $t$ and using $n^ne^{-n}\le n!$ gives the claim.
The same estimate at the endpoints implies that all derivatives extend
by zero there.

Periodize $\psi(\theta/\delta)$ and put
\[
 \mu_\delta=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\psi(\theta/\delta)\,d\theta
             =a_0\delta,\qquad
 g_\delta=\frac{\psi(\theta/\delta)-\mu_\delta}
                  {\delta(1-\mu_\delta)}.
\]
Take $\delta_0$ small enough that $\mu_\delta\le1/2$.
Then $g_\delta$ is even, has zero mean, satisfies $g_\delta(0)=\delta^{-1}$,
and has negative part bounded by $2a_0$.
The primitive $f_\delta(\theta)=\int_0^\theta g_\delta(v)\,dv$ is odd
and periodic. Its supremum is bounded by the integral of $|g_\delta|$,
which is uniform. Its positive-order derivative bounds follow from those
of $\psi$ and the displayed scaling. Choose one fixed $C_f\ge1$
to cover these profile bounds. A fixed cutoff $\chi$ is obtained
by integrating and normalizing $\psi$ to a smooth step and composing
that step with an affine function of $|y|^2$. Lemma~\ref{ipm:composition}
gives its Gevrey bound; the affine function is chosen to place the two
cutoff radii at $1/4$ and $1/2$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{ipm:profile-pressure-cost}
Let $m\ne0$, $\alpha,a\ge0$, and $\chi\ge0$, and consider the leading
pressure Hessian $(\nabla^2\pi)_{\rm lead}$ in
\eqref{ipm:intro-leading-matrices}, evaluated at $X(t,\ell y)$.
If $m_2>0$, then $(\nabla^2\pi)_{\rm lead}\le C_fc\alpha\chi a|m|I$.
Without a sign assumption on $m_2$, $\|(\nabla^2\pi)_{\rm lead}\|\le
C_f^2c\alpha\chi a|m|/\delta$. Thus wherever
$\alpha\chi a|m|/\delta\le C_*h$, the positive-Hessian cost
in the first case is at most $C_fC_*c\delta h$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
The conclusions follow from $0\le(m\otimes m)/|m|^2\le I$,
$|m_2|\le|m|$, and the bounds $f'_\delta\ge-C_f$ and
$\|f'_\delta\|_\infty\le C_f^2/\delta$ from \eqref{ipm:profile-bounds}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{ipm:tube-localization}
Assume the four-field parent class \eqref{ipm:parent-class} with
parameter $K$. In physical labels let $m=F^{-T}m_0$, $|m_0|=1$,
and normalize the transported scalar amplitude by $a(s,\cdot)=1$.
Recall from \eqref{ipm:amplitude-coefficient-definition} that
$c_A(t,z)=\mathfrak m(F(t,z)^{-T}m_0)\cdot\nabla P(t,X(t,z))$.
Then all the following conclusions hold uniformly for $t\in[0,S]$.
For an absolute exponent $A$,
\begin{equation}
 [c_A]_n\le A_c B_c^n(n!)^2,\qquad
 A_c+B_c\le K^{A},\qquad
 [m]_n\le K^{A(n+1)}(n!)^2.
 \label{ipm:expanded-cascade-amplitude-coefficient}
\end{equation} Whenever
$\ell K^{2A+3}\le\varepsilon_{\rm loc}\le1$,
the normalized amplitude $a(t,\ell y)/a(t,0)=e^{\Lambda(t,y)}$
satisfies, on $|y|\le1$,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:centered-amplitude-gevrey}
 [e^\Lambda-1]_{n,y}
       \le C\varepsilon_{\rm loc}C^n(n!)^2\quad(n\ge0).
\end{equation}
If $\varepsilon_{\rm loc}\le1/(20\pi)$,
then for $|y|\le1$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac12\le
 \frac{a(t,\ell y)|m(t,\ell y)|}{a(t,0)|m(t,0)|}
 \le2,
 \qquad
 \left|\frac{m(t,\ell y)}{|m(t,\ell y)|}
          -\frac{m(t,0)}{|m(t,0)|}\right|
       \le2\ell K^{2A+3}.
 \label{ipm:cascade-tube-comparison}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Using \eqref{ipm:parent-class}, $(n+1)^2\le4^n$,
and $K\ge2$, one extra spatial derivative gives
\[
 [F]_n\le\mathbf1_{\{n=0\}}+K^{n+2}((n+1)!)^2
       \le K^{n+3}((n+1)!)^2
       \le K^{n+3}4^n(n!)^2
       \le K^{3(n+1)}(n!)^2.
\]
The same holds for $F_t$ and $F_{tt}$, using the bounds on $X_t$ and
$X_{tt}$. Since $\det F=1$,
$F^{-1}=\operatorname{adj}F=(\operatorname{cof}F)^T$. In dimension two,
its entries are the entries of $F$ times $\pm1$, so $F^{-1}$ has the same bound.

Transport of the parent density $P$ gives $r\circ X=F^{-T}\nabla P(0)$.
The product bounds of Lemmas~\ref{ipm:word-products} and~\ref{ipm:shiftcalculus}
show that a product of a fixed number of fields of this class has
size and growth constants bounded by a fixed power of $K$.
Thus $r\circ X$ has that class.

The reciprocal bound has the same quantitative
property. If $g\ge b>0$ and
$[g]_n\le U V^n(n!)^2$, differentiating $g g^{-1}=1$ gives, for $n\ge1$,
\[
 [g^{-1}]_n\le b^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^n
          \binom nl[g]_l[g^{-1}]_{n-l}.
\]
Choose $R\ge4V\max(1,U/b)$. Induction using \eqref{ipm:shiftabsorption} and $\sum_{l\ge1}(V/R)^l\le2V/R$ proves
$[g^{-1}]_n\le2b^{-1}R^n(n!)^2$.
This is also a direct special case of Lemma~\ref{ipm:composition}.
Its constants are polynomial in $U,V,b^{-1}$, not exponential in
these quantities.

For $m=F^{-T}m_0$ with $|m_0|=1$, the identity $F^Tm=m_0$ gives
$|m|\ge\|F\|^{-1}\ge K^{-3}$.
Apply the preceding reciprocal argument to $|m|^2$ and then the
product bounds of Lemmas~\ref{ipm:word-products} and~\ref{ipm:shiftcalculus} to the rational symbol
$\mathfrak m$ in \eqref{ipm:velocity-symbol}.
These estimates give the coefficient bounds
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-amplitude-coefficient}.
The fixed exponent $A$ is uniform in $m_0$, the child carrier, the seed,
and the stage. This verifies the coefficient interface with the exact
layer proposition using only its inherited four-field class.

Write $a(s,z)=1$. Since $a_t+c_A a=0$, the growth at the origin
cancels in the identity
\begin{equation}
 \Lambda(t,y):=\log\frac{a(t,\ell y)}{a(t,0)}
       =-\int_s^t[c_A(\sigma,\ell y)
                              -c_A(\sigma,0)]\,d\sigma.
 \label{ipm:cascade-normalization}
\end{equation}
On $|y|\le1$, the mean-value theorem and $S\le1$ give
\begin{equation}
 \|\Lambda\|_\infty\le A_cB_c\ell,\qquad
 [\Lambda]_{n,y}\le A_c(\ell B_c)^n(n!)^2\quad(n\ge1).
 \label{ipm:expanded-cascade-centered-derivatives}
\end{equation}
The bound $\ell K^{2A+3}\le\varepsilon_{\rm loc}$ and
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-amplitude-coefficient} imply $\ell B_c\le1$ and
$A_cB_c\ell\le\varepsilon_{\rm loc}$. At this step we only require $\varepsilon_{\rm loc}\le1$. Then for $n\ge1$ the
last bound is at most $\varepsilon_{\rm loc}(n!)^2$.
The exponential composition estimate of Lemma~\ref{ipm:composition}
gives
\eqref{ipm:centered-amplitude-gevrey}.
One can retain the factor $\varepsilon_{\rm loc}$ directly in its
proof: every positive-order derivative term in the exponential
chain rule contains at least one derivative of $\Lambda$, and all
those derivatives have this factor; the remaining factorial
partition sum is bounded by $C^n(n!)^2$. The $L^\infty$ estimate is $|e^\Lambda-1|\le e^{\|\Lambda\|_\infty}\|\Lambda\|_\infty$.
The same calculation works with any lower time limit
$\sigma\in[0,S]$ in place of $s$. It therefore controls the full
centered propagator, in both directions of time.

For the phase comparison, \eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-amplitude-coefficient}
and $|m|\ge K^{-3}$ give
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:phase-spatial-derivative-bounds}
 |\nabla_{z}\log|m||\le\frac{|\nabla_{z}m|}{|m|}
       \le K^{2A+3},\qquad
 \left|\nabla_{z}\frac m{|m|}\right|
       \le\frac{2|\nabla_{z}m|}{|m|}\le2K^{2A+3}.
\end{equation}
Apply the mean-value theorem between $0$ and $\ell y$.
On $|y|\le1$, \eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-centered-derivatives}
and the choice $A_cB_c\ell\le\varepsilon_{\rm loc}$ give
\[
 \left|\log\frac{a(t,\ell y)}{a(t,0)}\right|\le\varepsilon_{\rm loc},
 \qquad
 \left|\log\frac{|m(t,\ell y)|}{|m(t,0)|}\right|
 \le\ell K^{2A+3}\le\varepsilon_{\rm loc}.
\]
Thus $\varepsilon_{\rm loc}\le1/(20\pi)$ makes their sum at most
$2\varepsilon_{\rm loc}\le1/(10\pi)<\log2$. Together with the direction derivative bound in \eqref{ipm:phase-spatial-derivative-bounds}, this proves
\eqref{ipm:cascade-tube-comparison}.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{ipm:packet-history-geometry}
Let $P$ be an exact parent on $[0,S]$, $S\le1$, with
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:history-parent-bounds}
 H(P)\le I,\qquad \|\nabla P\|_\infty\le C_{\nabla}h_{\rm par}.
\end{equation}
Suppose a layer activates at $s$, reaches angle
$\pi/2+1/x_{\rm tar}$ at the child target time $t_*$ with gain at least $e^{c_0x}$,
and is retained until $S=t_*+2W_{\rm next}$.
Assume its angle at the origin satisfies
$\beta(t)\in[1/(2x),\pi-1/(2x)]$ for $s\le t\le t_*$,
where $x_{\rm tar}\ge x$ is sufficiently large.
Normalize its leading density-gradient increment at the origin to magnitude
$h$ at the target time by
\begin{equation}
 \alpha a(t_*,0)|m(t_*,0)|/\delta=h.
 \label{ipm:cascade-target-normalization}
\end{equation}
Then the history estimate is
\begin{equation}
 \alpha a(t,0)|m(t,0)|/\delta
       \le e h e^{-c_0x},\qquad 0\le t\le s.
 \label{ipm:cascade-small-history}
\end{equation}
Assume in addition that
\begin{equation}
 2(1+2cC_{\nabla})(1+h_{\rm par})W_{\rm next}
 \le\frac1{4x_{\rm tar}},
 \qquad x_{\rm tar}\ell K^{2A+3}\le\frac1{20\pi}.
 \label{ipm:cascade-future-localization}
\end{equation}
Then $m_2>0$ throughout $\operatorname{supp}\chi$
on $[s,S]$.
With $\gamma(t)=\alpha a(t,0)$,
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{[0,S]}\gamma\le\Gamma:=\max(1,CK^A\delta h),
 \label{ipm:cascade-gamma-choice}
\end{equation}
and its earlier history satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \gamma(t)\le CK^A\delta h e^{-c_0x},\qquad t\le s.
 \label{ipm:cascade-gamma-history}
\end{equation}
All exponents are fixed, and the constants are independent of the stage.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
The target normalization \eqref{ipm:cascade-target-normalization} and the
activation-to-target gain give
$\alpha a(s,0)|m(s,0)|\le\delta h e^{-c_0x}$.
By \eqref{ipm:backwardray} and $S\le1$, for $0\le t\le s$,
\[
 \alpha a(t,0)|m(t,0)|
 \le e^{s-t}\alpha a(s,0)|m(s,0)|
 \le e\delta h e^{-c_0x}.
\]
This proves \eqref{ipm:cascade-small-history} without the additional
smallness conditions.

Now assume \eqref{ipm:cascade-future-localization}. Equations
\eqref{ipm:history-parent-bounds}, \eqref{ipm:backwardray},
\eqref{ipm:rayidentity}, and the matrix bounds
\eqref{ipm:matrixbounds} give
\begin{align}
 \alpha a(t,0)|m(t,0)|&\le e\delta h,
                   &&0\le t\le t_*,
                   \label{ipm:cascade-before-target}\\
 \alpha a(t,0)|m(t,0)|&\le\delta h
              \exp[C(1+h_{\rm par})W_{\rm next}],
                   &&t_*\le t\le t_*+2W_{\rm next}.
                   \label{ipm:cascade-after-target}
\end{align}
From the phase equation,
$|\beta'|\le\|M_P\|\le1+2cC_{\nabla}h_{\rm par}$.

For $t_*\le t\le S$, the angle at the origin satisfies
\[
 |\beta(t)-\beta(t_*)|
 \le\int_{t_*}^t|\beta'(\tau)|\,d\tau
 \le2(1+2cC_{\nabla}h_{\rm par})W_{\rm next}
 \le\frac1{4x_{\rm tar}}.
\]
Since $\beta(t_*)=\pi/2+1/x_{\rm tar}$,
\[
 \frac\pi2+\frac3{4x_{\rm tar}}
 \le\beta(t)\le\frac\pi2+\frac5{4x_{\rm tar}},
 \qquad
 \sin\beta(t)\ge\cos\frac5{4x_{\rm tar}}\ge\frac12
\]
for sufficiently large $x_{\rm tar}$. This same requirement bounds the exponential in
\eqref{ipm:cascade-after-target} by a fixed constant, which we may
take to be $2$ after tightening the fixed threshold.

For $s\le t\le t_*$, the assumed activation-angle range gives
\[
 \frac1{2x}\le\beta(t)\le\pi-\frac1{2x},
 \qquad \sin\beta(t)\ge\sin\frac1{2x}\ge\frac1{\pi x}.
\]
The second bound in \eqref{ipm:cascade-future-localization} controls the spatial direction error.

By \eqref{ipm:cascade-tube-comparison}, for $y\in\operatorname{supp}\chi$
and $s\le t\le S$,
\[
 \left|\frac{m_2(t,\ell y)}{|m(t,\ell y)|}-\sin\beta(t)\right|
 \le2\ell K^{2A+3}
 \le\frac1{10\pi x_{\rm tar}}\le\frac1{10\pi x}.
\]
Consequently, for $s\le t\le t_*$,
\[
 \frac{m_2(t,\ell y)}{|m(t,\ell y)|}
 \ge\frac1{\pi x}-\frac1{10\pi x}
 =\frac9{10\pi x}>0.
\]
For $t_*\le t\le S$, the retained-future angle bound instead gives
\[
 \frac{m_2(t,\ell y)}{|m(t,\ell y)|}
 \ge\frac12-\frac1{10\pi x_{\rm tar}}>0.
\]
This proves $m_2>0$ throughout $\operatorname{supp}\chi$ on
$[s,S]$. We use no phase sign during earlier history $[0,s]$.
\label{ipm:expanded-cascade-supported-sign}

The amplitude parameter also has a polynomial bound. Since
$\gamma(t)=\alpha a(t,0)$ and $|m(t,0)|^{-1}\le CK^A$,
\eqref{ipm:cascade-before-target} and \eqref{ipm:cascade-after-target}
justify the choice \eqref{ipm:cascade-gamma-choice}.
The history bound \eqref{ipm:cascade-small-history} similarly gives
\eqref{ipm:cascade-gamma-history}.
Neither $\alpha^{-1}$ nor the large raw amplification enters $\mathcal P$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{ipm:packet-budget}
Assume the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{ipm:packet-history-geometry},
with the profile of Lemma~\ref{ipm:profilelemma}, and set
$C_\chi=\max(1,\|\chi\|_\infty)$. For the leading fields
in \eqref{ipm:intro-leading-matrices}, for all $t\in[0,S]$ and globally in space,
\begin{align}
 (\nabla^2\pi)_{\rm lead}&\le C_H(\delta h+he^{-c_0x})I,
 \label{ipm:cascade-h-budget}\\
 \|(\nabla\Theta)_{\rm lead}\|_\infty&\le C_{\rm lead}h,
 \qquad C_{\rm lead}:=2eC_\chi C_f^2.
 \label{ipm:cascade-gradient-increment}
\end{align}
The constants depend only on the fixed bump $\psi$ and cutoff $\chi$,
and are independent of the stage.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Proposition~\ref{ipm:packet-history-geometry} and the localization bound
\eqref{ipm:cascade-tube-comparison} give, on the support of $\chi$,
$\alpha\chi a|m|/\delta\le Ch$ throughout $[0,S]$ and
$\alpha\chi a|m|/\delta\le Ch e^{-c_0x}$ on $[0,s]$.
On $[s,S]$, $m_2>0$, so Corollary~\ref{ipm:profile-pressure-cost}
gives $(\nabla^2\pi)_{\rm lead}\le C\delta hI$. On $[0,s]$, its sign-free bound
gives $\|(\nabla^2\pi)_{\rm lead}\|\le Ch e^{-c_0x}$. This proves
\eqref{ipm:cascade-h-budget}. The formula
$(\nabla\Theta)_{\rm lead}\circ X=\alpha\chi a\,m f'_\delta$ and
$\|f'_\delta\|_\infty\le C_f^2/\delta$ from \eqref{ipm:profile-bounds}, together with the target normalization
\eqref{ipm:cascade-target-normalization}, give
\eqref{ipm:cascade-gradient-increment}. Both leading fields vanish
outside the transported support, so the bounds hold globally.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{ipm:word-products}
The preceding word norms satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:wordproduct}
 E_n(fg)\le C_s\sum_{l=0}^n\binom nl E_l(f)E_{n-l}(g).
\end{equation}
For $D_n$ and coefficient multiplication, respectively,
\[
 D_n(fg)\le\sum_{l=0}^n\binom nl D_l(f)D_{n-l}(g),\qquad
 E_n(af)\le C_s\sum_{l=0}^n\binom nl
 \left(\sum_{|I|=l}\|\partial^Ia\|_{W^{s,\infty}}\right)E_{n-l}(f).
\]
The constants are independent of the torus periods.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{ipm:shiftcalculus}
For $n,d_1,d_2\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:shiftproduct}
 \sum_{l=0}^n\binom nl (l+d_1)!^2(n-l+d_2)!^2
 \le 3(n+d_1+d_2)!^2.
\end{equation}
For $0\le l\le n$ and every $d\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:shiftabsorption}
 \binom nl(l!)^2\frac{(n-l+d)!^2}{(n+d)!^2}\le\binom nl^{-1}\le1.
\end{equation}
Consequently, if $E_n(f)\le A_fE_{d_1}(n)$ and
$E_n(g)\le A_gE_{d_2}(n)$ for every $n\ge0$, then
\[
 E_n(fg)\le3C_sA_fA_gE_{d_1+d_2}(n),\qquad
 E_n(\nabla f)=E_{n+1}(f)\le A_fE_{d_1+1}(n).
\]
For an integer $q\ge0$ and a prefactor $B\le R^q$,
\[
 B E_d(n)\le E_{d+q}(n),\qquad
 \frac{E_{d+q}(n)}{E_d(n)}
 =R^q\left(\frac{(n+d+q)!}{(n+d)!}\right)^2\ge R^q.
\]
In particular, a prefactor $B\le Q^b$ is absorbed when $R\ge Q^r$
and $rq\ge b$, with $Q\ge1$ and $r>0$.
For a finite sum, if $E_n(f_j)\le A E_d(n)$ for $1\le j\le M$ and
$M\le R(d+1)^2$, then
\[
 E_n\!\left(\sum_{j=1}^M f_j\right)
 \le MAE_d(n)\le AE_{d+1}(n),\qquad
 \frac{E_{d+1}(n)}{E_d(n)}=R(n+d+1)^2.
\]
The same conclusions hold for $D_n$ using its product constant.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Increasing either $d_i$ shows
\[
 \binom{n+d_1+d_2}{l+d_1}\ge\binom nl.
\]
After division by $(n+d_1+d_2)!^2$, each summand in
\eqref{ipm:shiftproduct} is therefore at most $\binom nl^{-1}$.
For $n\ge4$, the sum of these reciprocals is bounded by
$2+2/n+2(n-3)/(n(n-1))<3$; the cases $n\le3$ are direct.
For \eqref{ipm:shiftabsorption}, the factorial quotient
$(n+d)!/(n-l+d)!$ increases with $d$. At $d=0$ the left side is
$\binom nl^{-1}$. The norm bounds follow from Lemma~\ref{ipm:word-products},
the identity $E_n(\nabla f)=E_{n+1}(f)$, and the displayed weight ratios.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{ipm:radius-calculus}
If $E_n(f)\le A R^{n+d}(n+d)!^2$, then, for $4\rho R\le1/2$,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:shifttoradius}
 \|f\|_\rho\le 2A(4R)^d(d!)^2.
\end{equation}
A fixed decrease of the radius also gives
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:radiusderivative}
 \|\nabla f\|_{\rho/2}
 \le C\rho^{-1}\|f\|_\rho,
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
The factorial inequality $(n+d)!\le2^{n+d}n!d!$ reduces
\eqref{ipm:shifttoradius} to a geometric series. Reindexing the
derivative sum proves \eqref{ipm:radiusderivative}, since
$\sup_n2^{-n}(n+1)^2<\infty$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{ipm:composition}
Suppose $a\ge\mu>0$ and $D_n(a)\le A R_0^n(n!)^2$.
Then
\[
 D_n(a^{-1})\le C\mu^{-1}
       [C R_0(1+A/\mu)]^n(n!)^2.
\]
If $D_n(f)\le A_oR_o^n(n!)^2$ and
$D_n(h)\le A_iR_i^n(n!)^2$, with $R_i,R_o\ge1$, then
for $g=\mathrm{id}+h$,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:compositionbound}
 D_n(f\circ g)\le C A_o R_{\rm new}^n(n!)^2,
 \quad
 R_{\rm new}=C\max\{R_i,R_o(d+2A_iR_i)\}.
\end{equation}
The constants depend only on the dimensions and norm conventions.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Differentiate $aa^{-1}=1$. For $n\ge1$, the derivative of the inverse
is bounded by $\mu^{-1}$ times the sum of terms with at least one
derivative on $a$. After division by
$\mu^{-1}R^n(n!)^2$, the induction for lower derivatives is closed by
\[
 \frac A\mu\sum_{l\ge1}(R_0/R)^l\le\frac12.
\]
One may take $R=C R_0(1+A/\mu)$. This argument applies directly to
word sums, so it introduces no order-dependent change of radius.

For composition, use the partition form of the chain rule. After
normalizing the $n$th derivative by $(n!)^2$, the term involving $q$
outer derivatives and positive block lengths $j_1+\cdots+j_q=n$
is bounded by
\[
 A_o R_o^q
 \frac{q!\prod_{i=1}^q j_i!}{n!}
 \prod_{i=1}^q\frac{D_{j_i}(g)}{(j_i!)^2}.
\]
Summing over ordered block lengths is an upper bound for the chain-rule
sum; the factor $1/q!$ for unordered blocks has already been included.
For positive $j_1,\ldots,j_q$ with sum $n$,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:composition-ratio}
 \frac{q!\prod_{i=1}^q j_i!}{n!}\le1,
\end{equation}
since the multinomial coefficient $n!/\prod j_i!$ is at least $q!$.
The coordinate and component sums are bounded by the corresponding
products of word sums.

Set
\[
 U(\zeta)=\sum_{j\ge1}\frac{D_j(g)}{(j!)^2}\zeta^j.
\]
Coefficientwise, the positive-order derivative generating series of
$f\circ g$ is bounded by
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:composition-series}
 A_o\frac{R_oU(\zeta)}{1-R_oU(\zeta)},
 \qquad R_oU(\zeta)<1.
\end{equation}
Moreover
\[
 U(\zeta)\le d\zeta+
               \frac{A_iR_i\zeta}{1-R_i\zeta}.
\]
At $\zeta=R_{\rm new}^{-1}$ with the fixed constant in
\eqref{ipm:compositionbound} large enough, $R_i\zeta\le1/2$ and
$R_oU\le1/2$. The value of the generating series is bounded by
$C A_o$, which bounds each nonnegative coefficient. The zeroth
derivative is bounded directly by $D_0(f)$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{ipm:smallflow}
Let $b(t,z)$ be smooth and periodic in $z\in\mathbb R^d$, and assume
\[
 \sup_{0\le t\le S}D_n(b(t))\le B R^n(n!)^2,
 \qquad SBR\le c_d,
\]
where $R\ge1$ and $c_d>0$ is a sufficiently small fixed constant.
Its flow from $\Phi(0,z)=z$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:smallflowbound}
 \sup_t D_n(\Phi(t)-\mathrm{id})
       \le C_dBS(C_dR)^n(n!)^2.
\end{equation}
The first time derivative satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:smallflow-first-time}
 \sup_{0\le t\le S}D_n(\Phi_t(t))\le C_dB(C_dR)^n(n!)^2.
\end{equation}
If also $\sup_{0\le t\le S}D_n(b_t(t))\le B_tR_t^n(n!)^2$, with $R_t\ge1$, then
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:smallflow-second-time}
 \sup_{0\le t\le S}D_n(\Phi_{tt}(t))
 \le C_d(B_t+B^2R)\bigl(C_d\max\{R,R_t\}\bigr)^n(n!)^2.
\end{equation}
These estimates hold for every $n\ge0$, with $C_d$ depending only on $d$.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
For a finite derivative cutoff $J$, define
\[
 U_J(t,\zeta)=d\zeta+
       \sum_{n=1}^J\frac{D_n(\Phi(t)-\mathrm{id})}{(n!)^2}\zeta^n.
\]
The preceding partition calculation and $\Phi_t=b\circ\Phi$ give
\[
 U_J(t,\zeta)\le d\zeta+
       \int_0^t\frac{BRU_J(\tau,\zeta)}{1-RU_J(\tau,\zeta)}\,d\tau
\]
as long as $RU_J<1$. This holds first on a short interval, since
$U_J(0,\zeta)=d\zeta$. Take $\zeta=(4dR)^{-1}$.
On the bootstrap interval $RU_J\le1/2$, Gronwall gives
$U_J\le d\zeta\exp(2BRS)$. Choosing $c_d$ small closes
$U_J\le2d\zeta$ with a strict margin. Substitution into the integral
inequality yields $U_J-d\zeta\le C BS$.
This proves \eqref{ipm:smallflowbound} for positive orders, uniformly
in $J$; the zeroth order follows from $|\Phi-z|\le BS$.

For $h=\Phi-\mathrm{id}$, \eqref{ipm:smallflowbound} gives size
$A_i=C_dBS$ and growth $R_i=C_dR$, so $A_iR_i\le C_d^2c_d$.
Thus \eqref{ipm:compositionbound} applied to $\Phi_t=b\circ\Phi$
gives \eqref{ipm:smallflow-first-time}.
Also, $(n+1)^2\le4^n$ and Lemmas~\ref{ipm:word-products}
and~\ref{ipm:shiftcalculus} give, uniformly in time,
\[
 D_n(\nabla_zb)=D_{n+1}(b)\le BR(4R)^n(n!)^2,
 \qquad
 D_n((\nabla_zb)b)\le C_dB^2R(4R)^n(n!)^2.
\]
Consequently
\[
 D_n\bigl(b_t+(\nabla_zb)b\bigr)
 \le C_d(B_t+B^2R)\bigl(4\max\{R,R_t\}\bigr)^n(n!)^2.
\]
Applying \eqref{ipm:compositionbound} to
$\Phi_{tt}=(b_t+(\nabla_zb)b)\circ\Phi$ proves
\eqref{ipm:smallflow-second-time}.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{ipm:packet-calculus}
Under \eqref{ipm:parent-class}, the fields in
\eqref{ipm:label-coefficients} and $F$, $F_t$, $F_{tt}$, $F^TF$, $G_t$, $(a_2)_t$, $(F^TF)_t$
expressed in the unscaled labels $z$ satisfy, for one fixed exponent $A_0$,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:derived-coefficients}
 \sum_{|I|=n}\|\partial_z^IC\|_{W_z^{s,\infty}}
 \le K^{A_0}(K^{A_0})^n(n!)^2,
 \qquad G\ge K^{-6}I.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
Derivatives in $y$ are more favorable because they bring powers of $\ell<1$.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
From \eqref{ipm:parent-class}, a fixed derivative shift and
$(n+j)!\le2^{n+j}n!j!$ show that $F,F_t,F_{tt}$ have size and growth
bounded by fixed powers of $K$. Since $\det F=1$ in dimension two,
$F^{-1}=\operatorname{adj}F=(\operatorname{cof}F)^T$ has the same bounds.
The product and reciprocal estimates of Lemmas~\ref{ipm:word-products},
\ref{ipm:shiftcalculus}, and~\ref{ipm:composition} give
\eqref{ipm:derived-coefficients} for all the listed fields, using
$(F^{-1})_t=-F^{-1}F_tF^{-1}$ to bound $G_t$ and $(a_2)_t$, and
$(F^TF)_t=F_t^TF+F^TF_t$.

The proof of Lemma~\ref{ipm:tube-localization} gives $\|F\|\le K^3$, so
$v^TGv=|F^{-T}v|^2\ge\|F\|^{-2}|v|^2\ge K^{-6}|v|^2$.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{ipm:meaninverse}
Under the parent hypotheses \eqref{ipm:parent-class} and
$\nabla^2p\le I$ on $[0,S]$, there are fixed exponents $A_1,A_2$ such
that the following holds for every $R\ge K^{A_1}$ and every integer
$d\ge0$. Suppose $f(t,y)$ is smooth, has zero spatial integral for
every $t$, and satisfies
\[
 \sup_{0\le t\le S}|f(t)|_n\le A E_d(n)\qquad(n\ge0).
\]
Then there are a choice of initial data and a solution $B$ of
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:mean-equation}
 B_t+(\mathcal V_{\mathrm{mean}}B)\cdot R_0=f
\end{equation}
both depending linearly on $f$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:mean-bound}
 \sup_t|(B,B_t,\mathcal V_{\mathrm{mean}}B,\nabla_y\bar q)(t)|_n
 \le K^{A_2}A E_d(n)\qquad(n\ge0).
\end{equation}
The same shift $d$ occurs on both sides. The selected
$B$ has zero spatial integral at every time. For an odd parent and odd
source it is odd. The constants are uniform as the torus is enlarged.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
\textbf{Step 1. Coefficient constants and multiplication.}
We first fix constants which will make the proof quantitative.
By the previously derived parent bounds, choose
$\mathsf M,\mathsf R\ge2$, both bounded by fixed powers of $K$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-coefficients}
 \sum_{|I|=n}\|\partial^IC(t)\|_\infty
 \le\mathsf M\mathsf R^n(n!)^2,
 \quad C\in\{F,F^TF,cF^Te_2R_0^T,R_0,G,a_2,c_A^{\ell}\},
 \quad \|F^{-1}\|_\infty\le\mathsf M.
\end{equation}
Increase them, still by fixed powers of $K$, to cover the first time
derivatives $G_t,(a_2)_t$ needed later.

To bound coefficient fields in $W^{s,\infty}$, use the elementary
fixed-shift estimate $(n+j)!=\binom{n+j}{n}n!j!\le2^{n+j}n!j!$ to obtain
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-multiplier-class}
 \sum_{|I|=n}\|\partial^IC\|_{W^{s,\infty}}
 \le L_s R_s^n(n!)^2,
 \quad R_s=4\mathsf R,
 \quad L_s=C_s\mathsf M(4\mathsf R)^s(s!)^2.
\end{equation}
Here $C_s$ depends only on $s$ and dimension; a finite sum over the
listed coefficient fields may be included by increasing $C_s$.
Write $\Lambda_s=C_sL_s$, increasing $C_s$ to cover all fixed component sums.
For every $R\ge2R_s$, every $d\ge0$, and every field with shifted input bound
$|v(t)|_n\le A_*E_d(n)$, the coefficient product estimate of
Lemma~\ref{ipm:word-products} and \eqref{ipm:shiftabsorption} give
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-multiplication}
 \begin{split}
 |Cv(t)|_n
 &\le\Lambda_s A_*E_d(n)
       \sum_{l=0}^n(R_s/R)^l
       \binom nl\frac{(l!)^2((n-l+d)!)^2}{((n+d)!)^2}\\
 &\le2\Lambda_s A_*E_d(n).
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
This formula shows explicitly that coefficient multiplication does
not enlarge the chosen $R$, even for an arbitrarily shifted input bound.

\textbf{Step 2. Scaling and the exact displacement identities.}
The relevant physical domain for the label variable $y$ is the
enlarged torus. On it introduce the rescaled parent
\[
 \widetilde P(t,\widetilde x)=\ell^{-1}P(t,\ell\widetilde x),
 \quad \widetilde u(t,\widetilde x)=\ell^{-1}u(P)(t,\ell\widetilde x),
 \quad \widetilde p(t,\widetilde x)=\ell^{-2}p(t,\ell\widetilde x).
\]
Its flow is $\widetilde X(t,y)=\ell^{-1}X(t,\ell y)$. Direct differentiation gives
\[
 \nabla_y\widetilde X(t,y)=\nabla_zX(t,\ell y)=F(t,y),\quad
 \nabla_{\widetilde x}\widetilde P(t,\widetilde x)
 =\nabla_xP(t,\ell\widetilde x),\quad
 \nabla_{\widetilde x}^2\widetilde p(t,\widetilde x)
 =\nabla_x^2p(t,\ell\widetilde x).
\]
Thus the rescaled equation has the same coefficient $c$, the same
upper Hessian bound, and initial gradient $R_0$, with no extra factor
of $\ell$. We suppress the tildes in the following identities only.

With the notation of Section~1.1, the parent identities
\eqref{ipm:raybasic} still hold after rescaling. For a linearized density
$\varrho$, introduce the displacement $\eta$ and
$Q=\varrho+r\cdot\eta$ as in \eqref{ipm:intro-displacement-definition}.
The calculation there gives the displacement system
\eqref{ipm:intro-displacement}.
We will construct a particular solution by imposing $Q(0)=0$ and
$\eta(S)=0$. These conditions select the initial density
$\varrho(0)=-r(0)\cdot\eta(0)$.

In parent labels, write $\eta\circ\widetilde X=Fz$ and denote the
pullbacks of $Q,f,\pi$ by $\widetilde X$ by the same letters. Since
$F_t=(M\circ\widetilde X)F$ and
$r\circ\widetilde X=F^{-T}R_0$, system
\eqref{ipm:intro-displacement} becomes
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:terminal-mean-system}
 \begin{aligned}
 Q_t&=f,\qquad Q(0)=0,\\
 \mathcal Kz_t-\mathcal Bz+\nabla_y\pi&=g,
 \qquad\operatorname{div}_yz=0,\qquad z(S)=0,\\
 \mathcal K&=F^TF,\qquad
 \mathcal B=cF^Te_2R_0^T,\qquad g=-cF^Te_2Q.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
To check the middle line directly, multiply the displacement equation
by $F^T$ after pullback. The coefficient of $z$ becomes
$F^T((M+H)\circ\widetilde X)F=-cF^Te_2R_0^T=-\mathcal B$.
The Piola identity gives
$(\operatorname{div}_x\eta)\circ\widetilde X
 =\operatorname{div}_y\bigl(F^{-1}(\eta\circ\widetilde X)\bigr)
 =\operatorname{div}_yz$, since $\det F=1$.

\textbf{Step 3. Existence and smoothness of the solution $z$.}
Let $\Pi$ be the $L^2$-orthogonal projection from
$L^2(\mathbb T_\ell^2;\mathbb R^2)$ onto the closed subspace
\[
 \mathcal H=\{v\in L^2(\mathbb T_\ell^2;\mathbb R^2):
 \operatorname{div}_yv=0\text{ in distributions}\}.
\]
This space includes constant vector fields; no zero-mean condition is imposed.
On $\mathcal H$ define
\[
 T_{\mathcal K}(t)v=\Pi(\mathcal K(t)v).
\]
For each $t$, $T_{\mathcal K}(t)$ is a bounded self-adjoint operator on $\mathcal H$, and
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-coercivity}
 \langle v,T_{\mathcal K}v\rangle
 =\|Fv\|_2^2\ge\mathsf M^{-2}\|v\|_2^2.
\end{equation}
Coercivity gives an inverse on $\mathcal H$ of norm at most
$\mathsf M^2$: its range is closed by the lower bound, and its
orthogonal complement is its kernel, which is zero. Since
$\Pi\nabla_y\pi=0$, applying $\Pi$ to the equation for $z$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-terminal-ode}
 z_t=T_{\mathcal K}^{-1}\Pi(\mathcal Bz+g),\qquad z(S)=0.
\end{equation}
This is an ODE with bounded time-continuous operators on $\mathcal H$.
Standard local well-posedness for ODEs in $\mathcal H$, followed by
continuation over $[0,S]$ using linearity and boundedness of the
coefficients, gives a unique $z\in C^1([0,S];\mathcal H)$.

We justify spatial regularity of $z$ before differentiating it.
The projection commutes with translations. If $T_{\mathcal K}v=h$,
the difference quotient in any coordinate satisfies
\[
 T_{\mathcal K}\delta_av
 =\delta_ah-\Pi((\delta_a\mathcal K)\tau_av),
\]
where $\tau_a$ is translation by $a$ in that coordinate. The $L^2$ operator bound for $T_{\mathcal K}^{-1}$ and the
$W^{1,\infty}$ bound for $\mathcal K$ give uniform
difference quotient bounds when $h\in H^1$, and hence $v\in H^1$.
Iterating this argument, or differentiating after each established
regularity step, proves $T_{\mathcal K}^{-1}:H^j\cap\mathcal H\to H^j$
for every fixed $j$. Its norm is a fixed polynomial in the coefficient
bounds through order $j$. The inverse is continuous in time, as
follows from the resolvent identity. Solving
\eqref{ipm:expanded-mean-terminal-ode} in each $H^j$ and using $L^2$
uniqueness identifies these solutions with $z$. Thus $z$ is smooth.

For this $z$, $g+\mathcal Bz-\mathcal Kz_t$ has zero Leray
projection (in particular, zero spatial mean), so it is a periodic
gradient. This gives $\nabla_y\pi=\nabla_y\bar q$.
Only the gradient is estimated in \eqref{ipm:mean-bound}; the zero-mean scalar potential exists
by Fourier series, but its norms are not needed.

\textbf{Step 4. Backward energy, including arbitrary finite families.}
The identities of the exact parent imply
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-matrix-energy}
 \operatorname{sym}\mathcal B+\tfrac12\mathcal K_t
 =F^T\operatorname{sym}\bigl((M+ce_2r^T)\circ\widetilde X\bigr)F
 =-F^T(H\circ\widetilde X)F.
\end{equation}
For an arbitrary vector source $g$, testing the equation for $z$ by
$z$ therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:backward-energy}
 \frac12\frac d{dt}\|Fz\|_2^2
 =-\int(Fz)^T(H\circ\widetilde X)(Fz)+\int z\cdot g.
\end{equation}
The pressure term vanishes by the divergence constraint. Since $H\le I$,
$E(t)=\|Fz(t)\|_2$ satisfies the lower differential inequality
$E'\ge-E-\mathsf M\|g(t)\|_2$, interpreted by regularizing the norm
at zero. Integrating backward from $E(S)=0$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-pointwise-energy}
 \|z(t)\|_2\le
 \mathsf M^2\int_t^S e^{\tau-t}\|g(\tau)\|_2\,d\tau.
\end{equation}
The projected ODE also gives, at the same time,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-pointwise-time}
 \|z_t(t)\|_2\le
 \mathsf M^2\bigl(\mathsf M\|z(t)\|_2+\|g(t)\|_2\bigr).
\end{equation}

We will apply the estimates to families of derivatives of $z$.
The required norm is $\sup_t\sum_\alpha$, whereas estimating each
member separately would give the larger $\sum_\alpha\sup_t$ on the
source. For a finite family $z^\alpha$ with sources $g^\alpha$, indexed
by $\mathcal A$, sum \eqref{ipm:expanded-mean-pointwise-energy} before
taking the time supremum. Together with
\eqref{ipm:expanded-mean-pointwise-time} and $S\le1$, this gives
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-family-L2}
 \sup_t\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal A}
   (\|z^\alpha(t)\|_2+\|z_t^\alpha(t)\|_2)
 \le I_0\sup_t\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal A}\|g^\alpha(t)\|_2,
 \qquad I_0=4e\mathsf M^5.
\end{equation}
The constant is independent of the cardinality of $\mathcal A$.

\textbf{Step 5. A fixed Sobolev base with explicit polynomial constants.}
The norm $\sum_{j=0}^s\sum_{|I|=j}\|\partial^Iv\|_2$ is equivalent
to $\|v\|_{H^s}$ with constants depending only on $s$ and dimension,
not on the torus period. This follows by comparing their Fourier
weights; the final finite $\ell^1$--$\ell^2$ comparison concerns only
the fixed number of base derivatives, since $s$ and the dimension are fixed.

Commuting a word $I$ through the equation for $z$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-commuted-system}
 \mathcal K\partial^Iz_t-\mathcal B\partial^Iz
       +\nabla\partial^I\pi
 =\partial^Ig+
 \sum_{\emptyset\ne J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,|I|\}}
 \bigl[(\partial^{I_J}\mathcal B)\partial^{I_{J^c}}z
       -(\partial^{I_J}\mathcal K)\partial^{I_{J^c}}z_t\bigr].
\end{equation}
Grouping the position subsets by their cardinality produces $\binom{|I|}{|J|}$. Terminal zero data and the divergence
constraint are preserved by differentiation.

For a finite family put
\[
 J_j=\sup_t\sum_\alpha\sum_{|I|=j}
  (\|\partial^Iz^\alpha(t)\|_2+
   \|\partial^Iz_t^\alpha(t)\|_2),
 \quad F_s=\sup_t\sum_\alpha\|g^\alpha(t)\|_{H^s}.
\]
Let $M_s=C_s\mathsf M\mathsf R^s(s!)^2$, chosen to dominate all
coefficient derivative sums through order $s$.
For $0\le j\le s$, the family estimate and the commutator identity give
\[
 J_j\le I_0\left(C_sF_s+
 M_s\sum_{l=1}^j\binom jl J_{j-l}\right).
\]
With $V_j=\sum_{i=0}^jJ_i$, it follows that
$V_j\le(1+2^sI_0M_s)V_{j-1}+C_sI_0F_s$.
Thus a valid joint $H^s$ inverse constant is
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-base-constant}
 I_s=C_s(s+1)I_0(1+2^sI_0M_s)^s,
\end{equation}
after increasing the fixed norm-equivalence constant $C_s$.
Explicitly, uniformly over finite families,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-family-Hs}
 \sup_t\sum_\alpha
   (\|z^\alpha(t)\|_{H^s}+\|z_t^\alpha(t)\|_{H^s})
 \le I_s\sup_t\sum_\alpha\|g^\alpha(t)\|_{H^s}.
\end{equation}
Because $s$ is fixed, $I_s$ is a polynomial in
$\mathsf M,\mathsf R$ and hence a fixed power of $K$.

\textbf{Step 6. Arbitrary additional derivatives at the same growth rate.}
Apply \eqref{ipm:expanded-mean-family-Hs} to
\eqref{ipm:expanded-mean-commuted-system} for all $|I|=n$, and use
\eqref{ipm:expanded-mean-multiplier-class} to bound the commutator sum.
Writing
$U_n=\sup_t|(z,z_t)(t)|_n$ and $G_n=\sup_t|g(t)|_n$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:mean-triangular}
 U_n\le I_s\left[G_n+
 \Lambda_s\sum_{l=1}^n\binom nl R_s^l(l!)^2U_{n-l}\right].
\end{equation}
The binomial coefficient follows from Lemma~\ref{ipm:word-products}.

Choose
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-common-R}
 R\ge R_{\rm mean}:=\max\{2R_s,8I_s\Lambda_sR_s\}.
\end{equation}
Suppose $G_n\le A_gE_d(n)$ for an arbitrary $d\ge0$.
We claim
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:mean-all-order-bound}
 U_n\le2I_sA_gE_d(n)\qquad(n\ge0).
\end{equation}
The case $n=0$ follows
from \eqref{ipm:mean-triangular}. In the induction step normalize a
commutator by $E_d(n)$ and use \eqref{ipm:shiftabsorption}. Its total
contribution is at most
\[
 2I_s^2\Lambda_s A_g
       \sum_{l=1}^n(R_s/R)^l
 \le\tfrac12 I_s A_g,
\]
after the harmless numerical enlargement in
\eqref{ipm:expanded-mean-common-R}. Adding the direct source gives a
strict bound below $2I_sA_g$. This induction is uniform in $d$ and uses
one fixed $R$, with no radius reduction at each inversion.

\textbf{Step 7. Reconstruction, zero mean, and parity.}
Define
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:mean-reconstruction}
 B=Q-R_0\cdot z,\qquad
 \mathcal V_{\mathrm{mean}}B=z_t,\qquad
 \nabla_y\bar q=\nabla_y\pi=g+\mathcal Bz-\mathcal Kz_t.
\end{equation}
To verify the second identity, use
$g=-cF^Te_2Q$ in the equation for $z$ to get
$\mathcal Kz_t=-cF^Te_2B-\nabla_y\bar q$.
Multiplication by $G=\mathcal K^{-1}$ gives precisely
\eqref{ipm:mean-darcy}, and $\operatorname{div}z_t=0$ gives its
constraint. Since $R_0$ is time independent,
$B_t=f-R_0\cdot z_t$, which proves \eqref{ipm:mean-equation}.

For the bounds, $Q(t)=\int_0^tf(\tau)\,d\tau$ satisfies
\[
 \sup_{0\le t\le S}|Q(t)|_n\le SA E_d(n)\qquad(n\ge0).
\]
Multiplication \eqref{ipm:expanded-mean-multiplication} gives
\[
 \sup_{0\le t\le S}|g(t)|_n\le 2c\Lambda_sSA E_d(n)\qquad(n\ge0).
\]
The arbitrary-shift estimate \eqref{ipm:mean-all-order-bound} gives
\[
 \sup_{0\le t\le S}|(z,z_t)(t)|_n
 \le 4cI_s\Lambda_sSA E_d(n)\qquad(n\ge0).
\]
Use \eqref{ipm:expanded-mean-multiplication} again in each term of
\eqref{ipm:mean-reconstruction} and in $B_t=f-R_0\cdot z_t$.
The total output amplitude is bounded, for example, by
\[
 C_c(1+\Lambda_s)^2(1+I_s)A,
\]
after a fixed enlargement of the numerical constant. It is a
polynomial in the prescribed parent constants. The same argument
also controls $G\nabla_y\bar q$ if desired. It requires no source time
derivative.

Since $\int f(t)=0$, one has $\int Q(t)=0$. The vector $R_0$ is the
gradient of the periodic rescaled initial density
$\widetilde P(0,y)=\ell^{-1}P(0,\ell y)$. Hence
\[
 \int R_0\cdot z
 =-\int\widetilde P(0,y)\operatorname{div}z\,dy=0.
\]
It follows that $B$ has zero mean, including at the selected initial
time, when $B(0)=-R_0\cdot z(0)$. The periodic physical Darcy velocity
then has zero mean, as follows by integrating its Darcy law.

For an odd parent $F,\mathcal K,\mathcal B,R_0$ are even under
$y\mapsto-y$. An odd $f$ makes $Q$ and $g$ odd. The Leray projection
commutes with reflection, and the coercive inverse preserves its
parity subspaces. Uniqueness of the ODE for $z$ with $z(S)=0$ gives odd $z,z_t$;
then $B$ is odd. The pressure gradient is odd and its zero-mean scalar
potential is even. This completes the proof of
Proposition~\ref{ipm:meaninverse}.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{ipm:scalar-inverse}
Assume the parent class \eqref{ipm:parent-class} and $\ell K^{2A+3}\le\varepsilon_*$,
where $\varepsilon_*>0$ is sufficiently small and $A$ is the exponent
in Lemma~\ref{ipm:tube-localization}.
Let $R_E=\max\{8,R_\chi\}$ be the extended propagator growth rate
constructed below. It depends only on the fixed cutoff and the Sobolev
index $s=3$, and is independent of $K$.
For every $R\ge2R_E$ and $d\ge0$, a smooth source supported in
$\operatorname{supp}\chi$ with
$|f(t)|_n\le\gamma(t)A_*E_d(n)$ has a unique zero-initial solution of
\[
 A_t+c_A^{\ell}A=f,\qquad A(0)=0,
\]
satisfying $|(A,A_t)(t)|_n\le K^C\gamma(t)A_*E_d(n)$.
The solution preserves support and zero angular mean.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Normalize by $\gamma(t)=\alpha a(t,0)$. Since
$\gamma'/\gamma=-c_A^{\ell}(t,0)$, the equation becomes
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:normalized-scalar-equation}
 \partial_t\!\left(\frac{A}{\gamma}\right)
 +\bigl(c_A^{\ell}(t,y)-c_A^{\ell}(t,0)\bigr)\frac{A}{\gamma}
 =\frac{f}{\gamma}.
\end{equation}
For $|y|\le1$ and any two times $\sigma,t\in[0,S]$, in either order,
set
\[
 L_{\sigma,t}(y)
 =-\int_\sigma^t[c_A^{\ell}(\tau,y)-c_A^{\ell}(\tau,0)]\,d\tau,
 \qquad E_{\sigma,t}=e^{L_{\sigma,t}}.
\]
By \eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-amplitude-coefficient}, the localization
condition with sufficiently small $\varepsilon_*$ gives
\[
 \ell B_c\le1,\qquad
 \ell A_cB_c\le\varepsilon_*K^{-3}\le\epsilon_0,\qquad
 \epsilon:=S A_c\ell B_c\le\epsilon_0,
\]
where $\epsilon_0$ is a sufficiently small absolute constant.
Using $c_A^{\ell}(t,y)=c_A(t,\ell y)$ and
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-amplitude-coefficient}, the mean value theorem gives
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-log-propagator}
 \|L_{\sigma,t}\|_\infty\le\epsilon,
 \qquad
 \sum_{|I|=n}\|\partial_y^I L_{\sigma,t}\|_\infty
 \le S A_c(\ell B_c)^n(n!)^2
 \le\epsilon(n!)^2\quad(n\ge1).
\end{equation}

We next bound the spatial derivatives of $E_{\sigma,t}=e^{L_{\sigma,t}}$
uniformly in $\sigma,t$. Take $\epsilon_0\le\log2$. In the normalized
derivative generating series, the positive derivatives of $L$ are
bounded by $U(\zeta)=\epsilon\zeta/(1-\zeta)$.
The partition estimate \eqref{ipm:composition-ratio} bounds the
positive derivative series of $e^L$ by
\[
 \frac{2U(\zeta)}{1-U(\zeta)}
 =\frac{2\epsilon\zeta}{1-(1+\epsilon)\zeta},
\]
since the outer derivatives of the exponential are at most two on
$|L|\le\epsilon$. Comparing coefficients gives
\[
 \sum_{|I|=n}\|\partial_y^IE_{\sigma,t}\|_\infty
 \le2\epsilon(1+\epsilon)^{n-1}(n!)^2
 \le2\cdot2^n(n!)^2\qquad(n\ge1).
\]
The zeroth-order bound is $E_{\sigma,t}\le2$. For the base derivatives
$0\le j\le s$, use $((n+j)!)^2\le4^{n+j}(n!)^2(j!)^2$, as in
\eqref{ipm:expanded-mean-multiplier-class}, to obtain
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:centered-propagator}
 \tfrac12\le E_{\sigma,t}\le2,
 \qquad
 \sum_{|I|=n}\|\partial_y^IE_{\sigma,t}\|_{W^{s,\infty}}
 \le C_s8^n(n!)^2\qquad(n\ge0)
\end{equation}
on $|y|\le1$.
To obtain global multiplier bounds, choose a fixed Gevrey cutoff
$0\le\chi_*\le1$, equal to one near $\operatorname{supp}\chi$ and
supported in $B_1$, with
\[
 \sum_{|I|=n}\|\partial_y^I\chi_*\|_{W^{s,\infty}}
 \le C_{\chi,s}R_\chi^n(n!)^2\qquad(n\ge0).
\]
Define the extended propagator
\[
 \widetilde E_{\sigma,t}(y)
 :=1+\chi_*(y)\bigl(E_{\sigma,t}(y)-1\bigr).
\]
It agrees with $E_{\sigma,t}$ near $\operatorname{supp}\chi$ and equals
one outside $B_1$. The product bounds of
Lemmas~\ref{ipm:word-products} and \ref{ipm:shiftcalculus} give
\eqref{ipm:centered-propagator} globally for $\widetilde E_{\sigma,t}$,
with $8$ replaced by $R_E:=\max\{8,R_\chi\}$ and an enlarged $C_s$.
These constants depend only on $s$ and the fixed cutoff, and are
independent of the parent, seed, time pair, and expansion depth.

For a source supported in $\operatorname{supp}\chi$, direct integration of
\eqref{ipm:normalized-scalar-equation} gives the solution with zero initial data:
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:oscillatory-inverse}
 A(t,y,\theta)=\gamma(t)\int_0^t
 E_{\sigma,t}(y)\frac{f(\sigma,y,\theta)}{\gamma(\sigma)}\,d\sigma.
\end{equation}
Since $E_{\sigma,t}f(\sigma)=\widetilde E_{\sigma,t}f(\sigma)$ on the source
support, the multiplier estimate applied inside the time integral gives,
for $|f(t)|_n\le\gamma(t)A_*E_d(n)$ and $R\ge2R_E$ with
$R_E=\max\{8,R_\chi\}$,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-scalar-bound}
 |A(t)|_n\le
 C_sS\gamma(t)A_*E_d(n)\sum_{l=0}^n(R_E/R)^l
 \le2C_sS\gamma(t)A_*E_d(n).
\end{equation}
To bound $A_t=-c_A^{\ell}A+f$, the scale condition
$\ell K^{2A+3}\le\varepsilon_*$ and the global coefficient bounds
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-amplitude-coefficient} give, on $\mathbb T_\ell^2$,
\[
 \sum_{|I|=n}\|\partial_y^Ic_A^{\ell}\|_\infty
 \le A_c(\ell B_c)^n(n!)^2\le(n!)^2\qquad(n\ge1),
\]
while $\|c_A^{\ell}\|_\infty\le A_c\le K^A$.
Including the fixed base derivatives by factorial splitting yields
\[
 \sum_{|I|=n}\|\partial_y^Ic_A^{\ell}\|_{W^{s,\infty}}
 \le C_sK^A4^n(n!)^2\qquad(n\ge0).
\]
The multiplication argument of \eqref{ipm:expanded-mean-multiplication}
therefore applies with coefficient growth rate $4$ whenever $R\ge8$.
Since $R\ge2R_E\ge16$, the bound for $A_t$ follows with the
same $R,d$ and only a fixed polynomial cost in $K$.
The operator is local in $y$, so support and zero angular mean
are preserved. The calculation
also bounds the leading homogeneous coefficient
$A_1=\gamma(t)\chi E_{s_0,t}f_\delta$ over its entire history.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{ipm:lifted-inverse}
For the parent coefficients on $\mathbb T_\ell^2\times\mathbb T$, let
$D=k^{-1}\nabla_y+m_0\partial_\theta$. For each smooth scalar $A$, the system
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:lifted-darcy}
 \mathcal V_D A=-ca_2A-Gp,\qquad
 \operatorname{div}_D \mathcal V_D A=0,\qquad
 p\in\mathcal H_D:=
 \overline{\{Dq:q\text{ smooth periodic}\}}^{L^2}
\end{equation}
has a unique pressure vector $p\in\mathcal H_D$ and velocity
$\mathcal V_D A$. Let $\Pi_D$ be the $L^2$-orthogonal projection onto
$\mathcal H_D$, set $T_G=\Pi_DG|_{\mathcal H_D}$, and define
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:darcy-projection}
 \mathbb P_G=I-GT_G^{-1}\Pi_D.
\end{equation}
For $R\ge K^C$ and $d\ge0$, the inverse and $\mathbb P_G$ preserve
bounds of the form $|v|_n\le A_*E_d(n)$, with the same $R,d$ and
with $A_*$ multiplied by at most $K^C$. Explicitly, for all $n\ge0$,
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 |A|_n\le A_*E_d(n)&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 |(p,\mathcal V_D A)|_n\le K^CA_*E_d(n),\\
 |v|_n\le A_*E_d(n)&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 |\mathbb P_Gv|_n\le K^CA_*E_d(n).
 \end{aligned}
\]
For $0<\rho\le K^{-C}$ and every integer $N\ge0$, they satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:darcy-gevrey}
 \|\mathcal V_D A\|_{\rho,N}\le K^C\|A\|_{\rho,N},
 \qquad
 \|\mathbb P_Gv\|_{\rho,N}\le K^C\|v\|_{\rho,N}.
\end{equation}
after a fixed enlargement of $C$.
Under the same condition $R\ge K^C$, if
$|(A,A_t)|_n\le A_*E_d(n)$ for all $n\ge0$, then
$|(p_t,(\mathcal V_D A)_t)|_n\le K^CA_*E_d(n)$ with the same $R,d$.
All constants are uniform in $\ell,k$ and in the resonances of $D$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
\textbf{Step 1. Coercive inversion and derivative bounds.}
At a lifted Fourier frequency $(\xi,j)$ put
$\eta=k^{-1}\xi+jm_0$. The orthogonal projection $\Pi_D$ onto
$\mathcal H_D$ has symbol $\eta\otimes\eta/|\eta|^2$ when
$\eta\ne0$, and zero when $\eta=0$. It has norm at most one on
$L^2$ and on every ordinary $H^s$, commutes with ordinary derivatives
and translations, and is independent of time. All these facts are
uniform in $k$, $\ell$, and the possible resonances.
Distributional $D$-divergence-free vectors are exactly
$\ker\Pi_D$. The pressure equation is therefore
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:coercive-darcy}
 T_Gp=-c\Pi_D(a_2A).
\end{equation}
For $p\in\mathcal H_D$,
$\langle p,T_Gp\rangle=\int p\cdot Gp\ge\lambda\|p\|_2^2$,
where $\lambda^{-1}\le K^C$. The closed-range/self-adjoint argument
used in \eqref{ipm:expanded-mean-coercivity} gives
$\|T_G^{-1}\|_{L^2\to L^2}\le\lambda^{-1}$.
This is an inverse on vector gradients; no scalar potential or
division by $|\eta|$ appears.

The difference quotient proof used above applies unchanged, because
$\Pi_D$ commutes with translations. Thus the inverse maps $H^j$ to
$H^j$ for every fixed $j$, and smooth right sides give smooth pressure
vectors. More quantitatively, for $T_Gp=\Pi_Dh$ one has
\[
 T_G\partial^Ip=\Pi_D\partial^Ih-
 \Pi_D\sum_{\emptyset\ne J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,|I|\}}
       (\partial^{I_J}G)\partial^{I_{J^c}}p.
\]
At the fixed base $s$, the same finite induction gives a valid
constant
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-elliptic-base}
 J_s=C_s(s+1)\lambda^{-1}
        (1+2^s\lambda^{-1}M_s)^s
\end{equation}
for the map $h\mapsto p$ on $H^s$, where $M_s$ is enlarged to cover
the fixed derivatives of $G$. The assertion holds for arbitrary
finite sums of input norms with no family-size factor, since the
estimate is instantaneous and can simply be summed.
Consequently the additional derivative norms satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-mean-pressure-triangular}
 |p|_n\le J_s\left[|h|_n+
 \Lambda_s\sum_{l=1}^n\binom nlR_s^l(l!)^2|p|_{n-l}\right].
\end{equation}
With $R\ge\max(2R_s,8J_s\Lambda_sR_s)$, exactly the same induction
as for \eqref{ipm:mean-triangular} gives
$|p|_n\le2J_s A_*E_d(n)$ whenever $|h|_n\le A_*E_d(n)$.
Apply the multiplication estimate to $h=-ca_2A$ and to $Gp$ in
\eqref{ipm:lifted-darcy}. This proves an arbitrary-shift bound for
$\mathcal V_D$ with the same $R,d$ and only a fixed polynomial prefactor.

The operator $\mathbb P_G$ in \eqref{ipm:darcy-projection} maps onto $\ker\Pi_D$, because $\Pi_D\mathbb P_G=0$, is the
identity on that kernel, and annihilates $G\mathcal H_D$.
The same estimates bound it on every shifted class. It need not be
orthogonal when $G$ is variable.

\textbf{Step 2. Finite-cutoff Gevrey bounds for the lifted inverse.}
The correction argument needs bounds for truncated derivative sums,
not just pointwise factorial estimates. Put
$\|v\|_{\rho,N}=\sum_{n=0}^N\rho^n|v|_n/(n!)^2$.
In the pressure commutator, the relevant weight identity is
\[
 \frac{\rho^n}{(n!)^2}\binom nl(l!)^2
 \frac{((n-l)!)^2}{\rho^{n-l}}
 =\frac{\rho^l}{\binom nl}\le\rho^l.
\]
Multiply \eqref{ipm:expanded-mean-pressure-triangular} by the weights
and sum. Each commutator index satisfies $n-l\le N$, so
\[
 \|p\|_{\rho,N}\le J_s\|h\|_{\rho,N}
 +J_s\Lambda_s\sum_{l=1}^N(\rho R_s)^l\|p\|_{\rho,N}.
\]
Choose $0<\rho\le(8J_s\Lambda_sR_s)^{-1}$, reducing the fixed upper
bound if necessary. The second term is absorbed. Multiplication by
a coefficient is bounded in the same finite sum, by Leibniz and
$\binom nl^{-1}\le1$. This proves \eqref{ipm:darcy-gevrey} uniformly in $N$.
Monotone convergence gives the full Gevrey estimates. The permissible
upper radius is a fixed inverse power of $K$; in the construction a
radius above a fixed inverse power of $\mathcal P$ is chosen beneath
it. This formulation separates the interval of valid radii from the
single radius eventually selected.

\textbf{Step 3. One time derivative without differentiating the hierarchy.}
The space $\mathcal H_D$ and the projection $\Pi_D$ do not vary in
time. Applying \eqref{ipm:seventh-foundations-time-inverse} from
Lemma~\ref{ipm:time-projected-inverse} with coefficient $G$, unknown $p$,
projection $\Pi_D$, and forcing $-ca_2A$ to
\eqref{ipm:coercive-darcy} gives
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:darcy-time-derivative}
 \Pi_D(Gp_t)=-c\Pi_D((a_2)_tA+a_2A_t)-\Pi_D(G_tp).
\end{equation}
The multiplier class was chosen to include $G_t,(a_2)_t$.
If $A,A_t$ have amplitude at most $A_*$ in one shifted class, its
right side has amplitude at most a fixed polynomial times $A_*$,
using the already established estimate for $p$.
Applying the same inverse once more gives $p_t$, and
\[
 (\mathcal V_D A)_t
 =-c(a_2)_tA-ca_2A_t-G_tp-Gp_t
\]
has the same $R,d$ and a fixed polynomial prefactor. The same assertion
holds for finite-cutoff Gevrey norms. It needs no $A_{tt}$ and no time
derivative of a recursive oscillatory pressure coefficient.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{ipm:common-growth}
There is a fixed exponent $r$ such that $R=\mathcal P^r$ is
admissible simultaneously for coefficient multiplication, the selected
mean inverse, the localized scalar inverse, the lifted Darcy inverse,
and its time derivative. Each operation returns the same $R$ and
the same arbitrary input shift, up to a fixed polynomial prefactor.
The choice is independent of the hierarchy depth.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
The preceding input and output requirements can be summarized as follows.
All polynomial factors below are independent of $n,d$ and the number
of times an operator is called.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.22\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.16\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.24\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.25\linewidth}}
\hline
Operation and reference & Required $R$ & Input bound & Output and cost\\
\hline
Coefficient multiplication, \eqref{ipm:expanded-mean-multiplication}
& $R\ge2R_s$
& $|v|_n\le A_*E_d(n)$; coefficient in $W^{s,\infty}$
& Same $R,d$; factor $2\Lambda_s$\\
Selected mean inverse, Proposition~\ref{ipm:meaninverse}
& $R\ge K^{A_1}$
& $|f|_n\le A_*E_d(n)$; zero spatial integral
& $B,B_t,\mathcal V_{\mathrm{mean}}B,\nabla\bar q$; same $R,d$, factor $K^{A_2}$\\
Supported scalar inverse, Proposition~\ref{ipm:scalar-inverse}
& $R\ge2R_E$ (independent of $K$)
& $|f(t)|_n\le\gamma(t)A_*E_d(n)$; supported in $\operatorname{supp}\chi$
& $A,A_t$; $A(0)=0$; same $\gamma,R,d$, factor $K^C$\\
Lifted Darcy and projection, Proposition~\ref{ipm:lifted-inverse}
& $R\ge K^C$
& $|A|_n\le A_*E_d(n)$ or $|v|_n\le A_*E_d(n)$
& $p,\mathcal V_D A$ or $\mathbb P_Gv$; same $R,d$, factor $K^C$\\
Time derivative of Darcy, Proposition~\ref{ipm:lifted-inverse}
& $R\ge K^C$
& $|(A,A_t)|_n\le A_*E_d(n)$
& $p_t,(\mathcal V_D A)_t$; same $R,d$, factor $K^C$\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The finite-cutoff bounds \eqref{ipm:darcy-gevrey} hold for
$0<\rho\le K^{-C}$, including the time-derivative bounds of
Proposition~\ref{ipm:lifted-inverse}.
Choose $R=\mathcal P^r$ to satisfy all thresholds in the table and
to dominate the fixed profile and cutoff growth constants.
Each threshold is a fixed polynomial in the prescribed parameters.
Increasing $r$ also permits fixed polynomial prefactors to be absorbed
into a fixed number of factorial shifts. This selection is made once,
before choosing a hierarchy order. Every inverse thereafter accepts
and returns precisely this $R$.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
No global $H^s$ norm of a nondecaying coefficient has been used in
these arguments. If such a norm were substituted on the enlarged
two-dimensional torus it could introduce a factor $\ell^{-1}$;
the multiplier formulation avoids that factor altogether. Pressure
potentials also have not been estimated: all coercive inverses act on
vector gradients. These two choices are what make the stated period
uniformity literal rather than an unrecorded scaling convention.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{ipm:hierarchy-construction}
Under the layer hypotheses, prescribe
$A_1(t,y,\theta)=\alpha\chi(y)a(t,y)f_\delta(\theta)$,
$B_1=\bar q_1=0$, and $\pi_0=C_0=0$.
At every finite depth $N$, smooth coefficients in
\eqref{ipm:raw-expansions} can be selected recursively as follows.
At each order $2\le p\le N$, first select the mean by solving
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:mean-recurrence}
 (B_p)_t+(\mathcal V_{\mathrm{mean}}B_p)\cdot R_0=-\langle Z_p\rangle
\end{equation}
then solve the oscillatory equation with zero initial data,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:oscillatory-recurrence}
 (A_p)_t+c_A^{\ell}A_p
   =-Z_p+\langle Z_p\rangle
      -(m_0\cdot \mathcal V_{\mathrm{mean}}B_p)\partial_\theta A_1,\qquad A_p(0)=0,
\end{equation}
and finally reconstruct the velocity and pressure by
\eqref{ipm:curl-recurrence}. Every mean source has zero spatial integral.
The finite velocity $v_{\mathrm{raw}}^a$ is exactly $D$-divergence free,
and substitution of the finite expansions into \eqref{ipm:raw-scalar}
cancels every power $k^{-p}$, $1\le p\le N$.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
\textbf{Step 1. Darcy reconstruction and incompressibility.}
In \eqref{ipm:curl-recurrence}, every coefficient multiplying an angular
function is independent
of $\theta$. Hence the right side defining $\pi_p$ has zero mean.
Also $m_0\cdot L=0$, so $m_0\cdot V_p=0$. Since $|m_0|=1$,
$V_p=m_0^\perp\partial_\theta\psi_p$. It follows that
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:exact-curl-pairs}
 k^{-p}V_p+k^{-p-1}C_p
      =k^{-p}D^\perp\psi_p.
\end{equation}
These pairs are exactly $D$-divergence free. The mean velocity is
also $D$-divergence free, since
$D\cdot \mathcal V_{\mathrm{mean}}B_p=k^{-1}\operatorname{div}_y \mathcal V_{\mathrm{mean}}B_p=0$.
Thus the finite $v_{\mathrm{raw}}^a$ is exactly incompressible, including its last
term $k^{-N-1}C_N$.

By \eqref{ipm:velocity-coefficient}, the field $E_p$ is known from the preceding oscillatory bundle,
has zero angular mean, and satisfies
\[
 m_0\cdot E_p=m_0\cdot C_{p-1}.
\]
In particular $E_1=0$. Substitution in
\eqref{ipm:curl-recurrence} also gives the coefficientwise Darcy identity
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-darcy-coefficient}
 V_p+C_{p-1}
   =-ca_2A_p-Gm_0\partial_\theta\pi_p
                      -G\nabla_y\pi_{p-1}.
\end{equation}
Indeed the right side is
$L(-ca_2A_p-J_{p-1})+C_{p-1}=V_p+C_{p-1}$.

\textbf{Step 2. Triangular recursion and scalar cancellation.}
In \eqref{ipm:raw-scalar}, the slow quadratic term has order $k^{-i-j}$ and the fast term
has order $k^{1-i-j}$. Thus at order $k^{-p}$ their sum is
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-order-count}
 \sum_{\substack{i+j=p\\i,j\ge1}}v_{\mathrm{raw},i}\cdot\nabla_y f_j
 +\sum_{\substack{i+j=p+1\\i,j\ge1}}
                 (m_0\cdot v_{\mathrm{raw},i})\partial_\theta A_j.
\end{equation}
The angular derivative annihilates $B_j$.
For $p=1$ the only possible quadratic term is
$(m_0\cdot v_{\mathrm{raw},1})\partial_\theta A_1=0$, so
$(A_1)_t+c_A^{\ell}A_1=0$, as prescribed.

For $p\ge2$ all indices in the slow sum are at most $p-1$.
In the fast sum the $i=1$ term is zero. For $i\ge2$,
\[
 m_0\cdot v_{\mathrm{raw},i}=m_0\cdot \mathcal V_{\mathrm{mean}}B_i+m_0\cdot C_{i-1}.
\]
The only current unknown in this expression is the term with
$i=p,j=1$ containing $B_p$. Thus every term of $Z_p$ in \eqref{ipm:full-source} is known before
solving order $p$. The unknown fast term has zero angular average.
First solve \eqref{ipm:mean-recurrence} using the selected mean inverse,
then \eqref{ipm:oscillatory-recurrence} using the localized scalar inverse,
and finally reconstruct the curl and pressure bundle by
\eqref{ipm:curl-recurrence}.
Angular averaging of \eqref{ipm:oscillatory-recurrence} preserves
$\langle A_p\rangle=0$. These equations cancel the coefficient of
$k^{-p}$ in \eqref{ipm:raw-scalar}.

\textbf{Step 3. Compatibility of every mean source.}

The curl pairs imply
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:coefficient-divergence}
 \operatorname{div}_y v_{\mathrm{raw},i}+
                  m_0\cdot\partial_\theta v_{\mathrm{raw},i+1}=0.
\end{equation}
In the first term only $V_i$ contributes: the mean velocity and
$C_{i-1}$ have zero slow divergence. In the second only $C_i$
contributes: $m_0\cdot V_{i+1}=0$ and the mean is angle independent.
The identity consequently holds without knowing the unknown
$A_{i+1},B_{i+1}$. Set $v_{\mathrm{raw},0}=0$; the $i=0$ identity is
$m_0\cdot\partial_\theta v_{\mathrm{raw},1}=0$.

In fact the compatibility holds locally in the slow variable:
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-local-flux}
 \langle Z_p\rangle
   =\operatorname{div}_y
       \sum_{\substack{i+j=p\\i,j\ge1}}\langle v_{\mathrm{raw},i} f_j\rangle.
\end{equation}
To check this identity, angular integration by parts changes the
fast sum into
$-\sum_{i+j=p+1}\langle(m_0\cdot\partial_\theta v_{\mathrm{raw},i})f_j\rangle$.
The divergence identity converts it to
$\sum_{i+j=p}\langle(\operatorname{div}_yv_{\mathrm{raw},i})f_j\rangle$.
Adding the slow sum gives the displayed divergence. The omitted
current mean drift and $E_p\cdot R_0$ have zero angular average.
All indices in the resulting flux are at most $p-1$, so the source
depends only on already constructed coefficients.

Integrate \eqref{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-order-count} over $y,\theta$.
Integration by parts changes its two sums into
\[
 -\sum_{i+j=p}\int(\operatorname{div}_y v_{\mathrm{raw},i})f_j,\qquad
 -\sum_{i+j=p+1}\int(m_0\cdot\partial_\theta v_{\mathrm{raw},i})f_j.
\]
Use \eqref{ipm:coefficient-divergence} in the second sum and replace
$i-1$ by $i$. Its $i=1$ contribution is zero, and the remaining sum
is the negative of the first displayed sum. Hence their sum vanishes.
The omitted current term
$(m_0\cdot \mathcal V_{\mathrm{mean}}B_p)\partial_\theta A_1$ has zero angular integral.
Also $\langle E_p\rangle=0$. It follows that
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-mean-compatible}
 \int_{\mathbb T_\ell^2}\langle Z_p\rangle\,dy=0.
\end{equation}
This is exactly the source condition needed by the selected mean
inverse. It is a cancellation of the full slow and fast sums, not an
assertion that each of their products separately has zero integral.

For comparison, the first nonzero mean source is explicitly
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:second-mean-source}
 \begin{aligned}
 \langle Z_2\rangle
 &=\langle A_1b_0\cdot\nabla_yA_1
            +(m_0\cdot C_1)\partial_\theta A_1\rangle\\
 &=\operatorname{div}_y\bigl(b_0\langle A_1^2\rangle\bigr).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
To check the coefficient, integrate the second term by parts in
$\theta$ and use
$m_0\cdot\partial_\theta C_1=-\operatorname{div}_y(b_0A_1)$.
Its sum with the first term is the divergence of $b_0A_1^2$.
Thus no factor of one half is missing.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{ipm:coefficients}
With $R,h$ chosen above and $a_p,b_p$ defined in \eqref{ipm:hierarchy-shifts}, every order satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:coefficient-bundles}
 \begin{aligned}
 (A_p,(A_p)_t,V_p,\pi_p,\psi_p,C_p,\nabla_y\pi_p)
       &\in[\gamma\Gamma^{2p-2};a_p],\\
 (B_p,(B_p)_t,\mathcal V_{\mathrm{mean}}B_p,\nabla_y\bar q_p)
       &\in[\Gamma^{2p-2};b_p]\qquad(p\ge2).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The same $R$ is used at every order. Each finite oscillatory coefficient
is supported in $\operatorname{supp}\chi$ in the label variable.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
The proof keeps separate the derivative shifts and the time profiles.
For $p=1$, write
$A_1=\gamma\chi E_{s_0,t}f_\delta$. The centered propagator has fixed
Gevrey bounds on this support, the cutoff is fixed, and the profile
has growth polynomial in $\delta^{-1}$. The fixed $H^3$ base adds
only a fixed prefactor and a fixed derivative shift. By the preceding
choice of $R,h$, both $A_1$ and
$(A_1)_t=-c_A^{\ell}A_1$ fit at shift $h=a_1-3h$ with profile $\gamma$.
The reconstruction costs at most $2h$, leaving $h$ shifts to absorb
the finite tuple sum within $a_1$. This proves the first bundle.

Assume all bundles through $p-1$ have been constructed. Split every
slow velocity into its oscillation $V_i+C_{i-1}$ and its mean
$\mathcal V_{\mathrm{mean}}B_i$, and split $f_j=A_j+B_j$. A fixed addition is included in the
operation cost above. With the larger shift $a_i$ permitted for a mean
factor, the following are bounds for the individual known sources:
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-cost-table}
 \begin{array}{c|c|c}
 \text{source}&\text{shift bound}&\text{time profile}\\ \hline
 E_p\cdot R_0&a_{p-1}+2h
                     =20hp-34h&\gamma\Gamma^{2p-4}\\
 \text{slow oscillation--oscillation},\ i+j=p
     &a_i+a_j+2h=20hp-30h&\gamma^2\Gamma^{2p-4}\\
 \text{slow mixed},\ i+j=p
     &a_i+a_j+2h=20hp-30h&\gamma\Gamma^{2p-4}\\
 \text{slow mean--mean},\ i+j=p
     &a_i+a_j+2h=20hp-30h&\Gamma^{2p-4}\\
 \text{fast curl},\ (i-1)+j=p
     &a_{i-1}+a_j+2h=20hp-30h&\gamma^2\Gamma^{2p-4}\\
 \text{fast mean},\ i+j=p+1
     &b_i+a_j+2h=20hp-22h&\gamma\Gamma^{2p-2}.
 \end{array}
\end{equation}
Absent factors with index one mean zero. The linear reconstruction
uses the preceding oscillatory bundle. In a fast curl product both
indices $i-1,j$ are at most $p-1$. In a known fast mean product
$i\le p-1$ and $j\le p-1$. Thus every line is covered by the induction.

Only slow oscillation--oscillation, slow mean--mean, and fast curl
terms can contribute to $\langle Z_p\rangle$. Indeed a slow mixed
term has one zero-mean angular factor and an angle-independent
factor; slow differentiation preserves that zero mean. A fast mean
term is a coefficient times an angular derivative. The linear
reconstruction has zero mean as well.
Since $\gamma\le\Gamma$ and $\Gamma\ge1$, the profiles of all
surviving mean terms are bounded by $\Gamma^{2p-2}$.
Their shifts are at most
\[
 d_M=20hp-30h=b_p-2h.
\]
There are at most a fixed constant times $p$ terms after the slow
products have been split into four types. For $p\ge2$,
$d_M\ge5hp$. Therefore
$E_{d_M+1}(n)/E_{d_M}(n)=R(n+d_M+1)^2$
absorbs this number of terms, including the fixed angular-average
constant, after the fixed choice of $h$. The mean source belongs to
$[\Gamma^{2p-2};d_M+1]$. Applying the mean inverse, with its zero
spatial integral established above, gives its entire bundle at shift
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-mean-reserve}
 d_M+1+h=b_p-h+1<b_p.
\end{equation}
This application is unweighted in time; no factor $\gamma(t)$ is
claimed for a terminally selected mean.

For the oscillatory equation, subtraction of the angle average
removes every pure mean product. Each remaining term retains at least
one oscillatory factor. The table and
$\gamma^2\le\gamma\Gamma$ show that its time profile is at most
$\gamma\Gamma^{2p-2}$. The current mean drift, now known, has shift
\[
 b_p+a_1+2h=20hp-22h=a_p-6h
\]
and exactly that profile. This is also an upper bound for every
known oscillatory-source shift in the table. Summing its at most
$Cp$ terms costs one shift. The scalar inverse costs at most $h$
more, so
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-osc-reserve}
 (A_p,(A_p)_t)\in
 [\gamma\Gamma^{2p-2};a_p-6h+1+h]
 \subset[\gamma\Gamma^{2p-2};a_p-4h].
\end{equation}
The scalar inverse is used only after the source has been bounded
pointwise by this profile. Division by $\gamma$ in its formula
cancels that existing factor; it does not introduce an inverse seed.

To finish the reconstruction, the current scalar bundle costs at
most $2h$ more, landing at $a_p-2h$. Inputs from order $p-1$ land at
most at $a_{p-1}+2h=a_p-18h$, with a smaller power of $\Gamma$.
The remaining $2h$ shifts absorb the fixed tuple sum and these two
input groups within $a_p$.
This proves \eqref{ipm:coefficient-bundles} with unit majorant
constant, rather than a hidden order-dependent constant.
Time derivatives in the two scalar bundles are supplied by the
inverse estimates or directly by their displayed equations; no
source or recursive pressure is differentiated in time.

Finally every oscillatory term is supported in the fixed label
support. Slow derivatives, angular primitives, and coefficient
multiplication preserve support. A global mean velocity enters an
oscillatory source only multiplied by an already supported
oscillation. The scalar equation is local in $y$ with zero initial
data. These facts prove the support assertion by the same induction.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{ipm:phase-parity}
At each fixed $(t,y)$ the coefficients $A_p,V_p,v_{\mathrm{raw},p},E_p$ have parity
$(-1)^p$ under $\theta\mapsto-\theta$, while
$\pi_p,\psi_p,C_p$ have parity $(-1)^{p+1}$.
Every odd-order mean $B_p$ vanishes.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
With $t,y$ fixed, $A_1$ is
odd in $\theta$. Inductively $A_p,V_p,v_{\mathrm{raw},p},E_p$ have parity $(-1)^p$,
and $\pi_p,\psi_p,C_p$ have parity $(-1)^{p+1}$. Slow sources with
$i+j=p$ and fast sources with $i+j=p+1$ both have parity $(-1)^p$,
the latter because $\partial_\theta$ reverses parity. At odd $p$ the
mean source is therefore zero, and the selected inverse returns
$B_p=0$. This observation is not needed for the estimates, but
independently checks their order indexing.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{ipm:finite-approximation}
Choose the common growth constant and the coefficients above. There
are fixed $\vartheta>0$, $Q,C,k_*>0$ such that, if
$\mathcal P^Q\le k^{\vartheta/100}$ and $k\ge k_*$, the depth
$N=\lfloor k^\vartheta\rfloor$ gives a normalized approximation
$A^a=k w^a$ with the following properties at a radius
$\rho\ge\mathcal P^{-C}$.
The approximation and its lifted transport obey
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:approximation-bounds}
 \begin{aligned}
 \|A^a\|_\rho+\|DA^a\|_\rho+\|(A^a)_t\|_\rho
       &\le\mathcal P^C,\\
 \|\mathcal V_D A^a-V_1\|_\rho&\le k^{-1}\mathcal P^C,\\
 \|b^a\|_\rho&\le k^{-1}\mathcal P^C\le k^{-1/2},
 \qquad b^a=(k^{-1} \mathcal V_D A^a,m_0\cdot \mathcal V_D A^a).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Its scalar residual satisfies
\[
 \|(A^a)_t+\mathcal V_D A^a\cdot R_0+
                   \mathcal V_D A^a\cdot DA^a\|_\rho
 \le \exp(-0.6k^\vartheta\log k).
\]
The finite oscillatory tail and phase means satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-split-tail}
 \begin{aligned}
 \left\|\sum_{p=2}^Nk^{1-p}A_p(t)\right\|_\rho
      &\le k^{-1}\gamma(t)\mathcal P^C,\\
 \left\|\sum_{p=2}^Nk^{1-p}B_p(t)\right\|_\rho
      &\le k^{-1}\mathcal P^C.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
All constants are independent of $N$ and the stage.
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
\textbf{Step 1. Darcy residual and velocity correction.}
We now keep a finite depth $N\ge2$. Its terminal curl term is essential:
as noted above, $v_{\mathrm{raw},N+1}=C_N$, even though there is no density
coefficient $f_{N+1}$. Summing
\eqref{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-darcy-coefficient}, and using
$\mathcal V_{\mathrm{mean}}B_p=-ca_2B_p-G\nabla_y\bar q_p$, gives the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:darcy-defect}
 v_{\mathrm{raw}}^a+ca_2w^a+
 G(\nabla_y+km_0\partial_\theta)q^a
       =k^{-N-1}H_N,\qquad H_N=C_N+G\nabla_y\pi_N.
\end{equation}
All lower powers cancel. The scalar pressure term is in the range
of $G D$, and $v_{\mathrm{raw}}^a$ is exactly $D$-divergence free. The projection
$\mathbb P_G$ annihilates $G D$ and is the identity on
$D$-divergence-free vectors. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:exact-darcy-correction}
 \mathcal V_D(w^a)-v_{\mathrm{raw}}^a=-k^{-N-1}\mathbb P_GH_N.
\end{equation}
The same-radius vector estimate for $\mathbb P_G$ is used here.
No scalar pressure potential, inverse carrier, or small-divisor
estimate is used to obtain this velocity error.

\textbf{Step 2. A quantitative majorant at one fixed radius.}

If $f\in[g;d]$, then at $\rho\le(8R)^{-1}$,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:shifted-to-radius}
 \|f(t)\|_\rho\le2g(t)(4R)^d(d!)^2.
\end{equation}
Indeed $(n+d)!\le2^{n+d}n!d!$ reduces the defining sum to
$g(4R)^d(d!)^2\sum_n(4\rho R)^n$.
Choose $\rho$ once smaller if needed to lie in the interval for
the exact Darcy/projection estimates. All choices are by fixed
powers of $\mathcal P$. The same argument may be applied after a
fixed number of derivatives by adding that fixed number to $d$.

By \eqref{ipm:coefficient-bundles}, since $a_p,b_p\le20hp$,
$R=\mathcal P^r$, and $\Gamma\le\mathcal P$, there are fixed constants
$A,D,C\ge1$ such
that every coefficient bundle, and its first two additional
spatial/angular derivatives when needed, obeys
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:order-coefficient-bound}
 M_p:=\bigl[\mathcal P^A(C(p+1))^D\bigr]^{p+1}.
\end{equation}
Here an oscillatory coefficient or its time derivative has the
stronger bound $\gamma(t)M_p$, whereas a mean coefficient has
bound $M_p$. All powers $\Gamma^{2p-2}$ have been absorbed into
$\mathcal P^{A(p+1)}$. To see that $A,D$ are fixed, the factors
$(4R)^{20hp+O(1)}$ are $\mathcal P^{O(p+1)}$, and
$((20hp+O(1))!)^2$ is bounded by
$(C(p+1))^{D(p+1)}$. Fixed coefficient operations and the projection
prefactor only enlarge these fixed constants.
