Keep the constant $C$ in $M_p$ fixed and define the larger constant
$C_B=e^2C$. Set
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-BN}
 \mathcal B_N=\mathcal P^A(C_B(N+2))^D.
\end{equation}
It may be chosen so that for $1\le p\le N$,
\[
 M_p\le\mathcal B_N^{p+1},\qquad
 M_{p+1}/M_p\le\mathcal B_N\quad(p<N).
\]
For the second inequality use
\[
 (p+2)\log(p+2)-(p+1)\log(p+1)\le\log(p+2)+1;
\]
the extra fixed factor $e^D$ is absorbed by $(C_B/C)^D$.

Choose the fixed $\vartheta>0$ sufficiently small that
$D\vartheta<1/200$, and put $N=\lfloor k^\vartheta\rfloor$.
Choose $Q$ after $A,D$ and all subsequent fixed polynomial exponents.
Under $\mathcal P^Q\le k^{\vartheta/100}$, for all sufficiently
large $k$ one has
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-ratio-small}
 \mathcal B_N\le k^{1/50},\qquad
 k^{-1}\mathcal B_N\le k^{-49/50}\le\tfrac12.
\end{equation}
Indeed
$\log\mathcal B_N\le A\vartheta\log k/(100Q)
 +D\vartheta\log k+O(1)$.

\textbf{Step 3. Low orders and the amplitude-weighted tail.}

Set $A^a=kw^a$. The normalized $p$th term is bounded by
$k^{1-p}M_p$, with an extra factor $\gamma(t)$ in its
oscillatory part. For $p\ge2$ the ratio of these majorants is at most
$k^{-1}\mathcal B_N$. Therefore, estimating $p=1$ separately and
$p=2$ with its own fixed value of $M_2$, the oscillatory and mean tails are bounded by
$2k^{-1}\gamma(t)M_2$ and $2k^{-1}M_2$, respectively. Since
$M_2$ is a fixed power of $\mathcal P$, this proves
\eqref{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-split-tail}.
The same estimates hold for the time derivatives in the supplied
bundles and for the fixed extra derivatives already included in
$M_p$. In particular
$\|A^a\|_\rho+\|DA^a\|_\rho+\|(A^a)_t\|_\rho\le\mathcal P^{C_2}$.
No bound by $\mathcal B_N^{O(1)}$ is inserted into $M_1$ or $M_2$;
doing so would unnecessarily make the low-order estimates depend
on $N$.

From the normalized finite velocity,
\[
 kv_{\mathrm{raw}}^a
 =V_1+k^{-1} C_1+
   \sum_{p=2}^Nk^{1-p}(V_p+\mathcal V_{\mathrm{mean}}B_p)
   +\sum_{p=2}^Nk^{-p} C_p,
\]
the same geometric estimate gives
$\|kv_{\mathrm{raw}}^a-V_1\|_\rho\le k^{-1}\mathcal P^{C_3}$.
The normalized exact Darcy correction in
\eqref{ipm:exact-darcy-correction} is
$-k^{-N}\mathbb P_GH_N$ and satisfies this bound as well.
For example, its norm is at most
$k^{-N}\mathcal P^C\gamma M_N$, and
$k^{1-N}M_N\le k^{-1} M_2$ for $N\ge2$.
The additional fixed powers of $\mathcal P$ and $\gamma\le\Gamma$
are absorbed in $C_3$. We have proved \eqref{ipm:approximation-bounds}.
In the last line $m_0\cdot V_1=0$ is used exactly, not estimated.
The last inequality follows by increasing the final fixed $Q$.
These estimates retain $\gamma$ on the finite oscillatory density
tail, but do not claim it for the phase means.

\textbf{Step 4. The entire residual, including the top curl.}

Let $\mathcal S_N$ denote the left side of
\eqref{ipm:raw-scalar} evaluated at $(w^a,v_{\mathrm{raw}}^a)$.
The recurrence cancels its coefficients at powers $1,\ldots,N$.
The remaining linear term is
$k^{-N-1}C_N\cdot R_0$. Its slow products have
$1\le i\le N+1$, $1\le j\le N$ and power $q=i+j$.
Its fast products have the same ranges and power $q=i+j-1$;
the $i=1$ fast terms are zero. In particular the fast product
with $i=N+1,j=1$ starts at power $N+1$, and has not been lost.
It follows that
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-raw-residual}
 \mathcal S_N=\sum_{q=N+1}^{2N+1}k^{-q} S_q.
\end{equation}

We now bound each $S_q$ without assuming any further cancellation.
For $i\le N$ the norm of $v_{\mathrm{raw},i}$ is at most a fixed polynomial in
$\mathcal P$ times $\mathcal B_N^{i+1}$; the same bound holds for
$v_{\mathrm{raw},N+1}=C_N$ with $i=N+1$. Each differentiated $f_j$ has bound
at most $\mathcal P^C\mathcal B_N^{j+1}$.
A slow product of power $q$ is therefore at most
$\mathcal P^C\mathcal B_N^{q+2}$, and a fast product at most
$\mathcal P^C\mathcal B_N^{q+3}$. The factors $\gamma$ can be
bounded by $\Gamma\le\mathcal P$ here: a residual need not retain
an oscillatory profile because it includes pure mean products.
There are at most $2N+1$ terms at any power. Since
$\mathcal B_N\ge N$ after increasing the fixed constants, their
number costs at most two additional powers. The linear term has
a smaller bound. Hence
\[
 \|S_q\|_\rho\le\mathcal P^{C_4}\mathcal B_N^{q+5},
 \qquad N+1\le q\le2N+1.
\]
Products with $R_0$ use its coefficient norm. This avoids inserting
the enlarged torus volume into the estimate.
By \eqref{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-ratio-small},
\[
 \|\mathcal S_N\|_\rho
   \le2\mathcal P^{C_4}k^{-N-1}\mathcal B_N^{N+6}.
\]

Replacing $v_{\mathrm{raw}}^a$ by the exact Darcy velocity adds exactly
\[
 -k^{-N-1}\mathbb P_GH_N\cdot
       [R_0+(\nabla_y+km_0\partial_\theta)w^a]
 =-k^{-N-1}\mathbb P_GH_N\cdot[R_0+DA^a].
\]
Multiplication by $R_0$ uses its coefficient Gevrey norm, whereas
$DA^a$ uses its function Gevrey norm. The corresponding product
bound is a fixed polynomial by the low-order estimates already
proved. The correction consequently has norm
at most $k^{-N-1}\mathcal P^{C_5}\mathcal B_N^{N+1}$.
Multiply the raw density equation by $k$ and use linearity of
$\mathcal V_D$. This gives
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:approximate-residual}
 r^a=(A^a)_t+\mathcal V_D A^a\cdot R_0+
                          \mathcal V_D A^a\cdot DA^a
\end{equation}
and the bound
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:residual-before-separation}
 \|r^a\|_\rho
       \le\mathcal P^{C_6}k^{-N}\mathcal B_N^{N+6}.
\end{equation}
All constants here are fixed independently of $N,k$ and the stage.
The source time derivative never enters this computation:
$\partial_t w^a$ uses only the time derivatives already in the
coefficient bundles.

Finally take logarithms. By
\eqref{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-ratio-small}, the right side of
\eqref{ipm:residual-before-separation} has logarithm at most
\[
 C_6\log\mathcal P-N\log k+\frac{N+6}{50}\log k.
\]
The first term is at most
$C_6\vartheta\log k/(100Q)$.
For sufficiently large $k$, this upper bound is less than
$-0.9N\log k$. Also
$N=\lfloor k^\vartheta\rfloor\ge0.9k^\vartheta$ once $k$ is large.
Thus, with ample numerical margin,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:tiny-residual}
 \|r^a\|_\rho\le\exp(-0.6k^\vartheta\log k).
\end{equation}
The radius remains at least a fixed inverse power of $\mathcal P$.
The entire finite hierarchy has used one unchanged growth constant
$R$; the fixed passage to this radius is performed only after its
construction. These are precisely the residual and approximate-field
bounds used in the next subsection.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{ipm:correction}
Suppose the supplied coefficients and approximation are real and
$C^\infty$ in time and space, the coefficients satisfy
\eqref{ipm:derived-coefficients},
and the approximation satisfies \eqref{ipm:tiny-residual} and
\eqref{ipm:approximation-bounds} at a radius bounded below by a fixed
inverse power of $\mathcal P$. Assume the frequency separation
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:correction-frequency-separation}
 \mathcal P^Q\le k^{\vartheta/100},\qquad k\ge k_*,
\end{equation}
for sufficiently large fixed $Q$ and $k_*$. Then there is a real
smooth $e$ on $[0,S]$ with $e(0)=0$ such that
$A=A^a+e$ solves
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:exact-lifted-scalar}
 A_t+\mathcal V_D A\cdot R_0+\mathcal V_D A\cdot DA=0.
\end{equation}
For one radius $\rho_*\ge\mathcal P^{-C}$,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:correction-bound}
 \sup_{t\in[0,S]}
 \bigl(\|e(t)\|_{\rho_*}+\|e_t(t)\|_{\rho_*}
                   +\|\mathcal V_D e(t)\|_{\rho_*}\bigr)
 \le\mathcal P^C\exp(-k^\vartheta/2).
\end{equation}
If the parent coefficients and approximation have the joint reflection
parities of the construction, the correction is odd under that reflection.
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
\textbf{Step 1. The exact error equation.}
Linearity of $\mathcal V_D$ gives
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal V_D(A^a+e)\cdot D(A^a+e)
       -\mathcal V_D A^a\cdot DA^a
 &=\mathcal V_D(A^a+e)\cdot De+\mathcal V_D e\cdot DA^a.
\end{align*}
Subtracting the residual of $A^a$ therefore yields exactly
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:correction-equation}
 e_t+b\cdot\nabla_{y,\theta}e+Z(e)=-r^a,\qquad e(0)=0,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-correction-bz}
 \begin{split}
 b&=b^a+T_{k^{-1}} e,\qquad
 T_{k^{-1}} f=(k^{-1} \mathcal V_D f,m_0\cdot \mathcal V_D f),\\
 Z(e)&=\mathcal V_D e\cdot(R_0+DA^a).
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
Since $\operatorname{div}_D \mathcal V_D f=0$,
$\operatorname{div}_{y,\theta}b=0$. In $Z(e)$, the derivative
$D$ falls on the given approximation, not on $e$. Thus $Z$ is a
linear operator of order zero on the correction.

Choose $\rho_0$ below the approximation and coefficient radii and
below the finite-cutoff Darcy threshold. There is a number
$\Lambda=\mathcal P^{C_0}\ge1$, with a fixed exponent $C_0$, such that
for every $0<\rho\le\rho_0$ and every cutoff $M$,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-correction-operator}
 \begin{split}
 \|\mathcal V_D f\|_{\rho,M}+\|T_{k^{-1}} f\|_{\rho,M}
       &\le\Lambda\|f\|_{\rho,M},\\
 \|Z(f)\|_{\rho,M}&\le\Lambda\|f\|_{\rho,M},\\
 \|b^a\|_{\rho,M}&\le B_0:=k^{-1/2},\qquad
 \|r^a\|_{\rho,M}\le R_k:=e^{-0.6k^\vartheta\log k}.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
Here $\|f\|_{\rho,M}=\sum_{n=0}^M\rho^n|f|_n/(n!)^2$.
For the term $R_0\cdot \mathcal V_D f$, use the coefficient norm
\[
 \sum_{l\ge0}\frac{\rho^l}{(l!)^2}
       \sum_{|I|=l}\|\partial^IR_0\|_{W^{s,\infty}}.
\]
It is bounded by a fixed power of $\mathcal P$ and multiplies the
$H^s$ norm of $\mathcal V_D f$. This avoids a volume factor. If one instead
places $R_0$ in unnormalized $H^s$, the cost is $O(\ell^{-1})$, also
allowed by $\mathcal P$. The term containing $DA^a$ uses the
$H^s$ algebra inequality. Both estimates hold at the same finite
cutoff by the factorial product inequality.

\par\smallskip\noindent\textbf{Step 2. The fixed Sobolev-base transport estimate.}
For a divergence-free vector field $b$ and a smooth scalar $v$ on
the three-dimensional lifted torus, $s=3$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-correction-base}
 \left|\sum_{|\beta|\le s}
 \langle\partial^\beta v,
            [\partial^\beta,b\cdot\nabla]v\rangle_{L^2}\right|
       \le C_s\|b\|_{H^s}\|v\|_{H^s}^2.
\end{equation}
Indeed, the term $b\cdot\nabla\partial^\beta v$ has zero pairing
with $\partial^\beta v$. In a remaining term let
$r=|\beta|\le s$ and $q\ge1$ be the number of derivatives on $b$.
For $q=1$, put $\partial b$ in $L^\infty$ and the derivative of
$v$ of order $r$ in $L^2$. For $q=r$, put $\partial^r b$ in $L^2$
and $\nabla v$ in $L^\infty$. These are controlled by $H^s$ since
$s>5/2$. For $1<q<r$, the available regularities of the two
factors are $s-q$ and $s-r+q-1$. Their sum is
$2s-r-1\ge s-1>3/2$, and both are nonnegative, so their product
is in $L^2$ with norm at most $C_s\|b\|_{H^s}\|v\|_{H^s}$.
There are finitely many base multi-indices, depending only on $s$.
This proves \eqref{ipm:expanded-correction-base}.

Apply this estimate with $v=\partial^I e$, for any additional
ordered word $I$. Its constant is independent of the length of $I$,
because only the fixed $s$ derivatives are expanded in this step.
In particular, it does not ask for $b$ in $H^{s+1}$ or for an
additional derivative of $v$ beyond $H^s$.

\par\smallskip\noindent\textbf{Step 3. All additional derivatives stay inside the cutoff.}
After applying a word $I$ of length $n$ to \eqref{ipm:correction-equation},
the transport terms other than $b\cdot\nabla\partial^I e$ are
\[
 \sum_{\varnothing\ne J\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}}
       (\partial^{I_J}b)\cdot
             \nabla\partial^{I_{J^c}}e.
\]
For $|J|=l\ge1$ put $j=n-l$. Summing over the coordinate choices
in $I$ and over subsets of size $l$ gives the bound
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-correction-wordcount}
 C_s\binom nl |b|_l\,|e|_{j+1}.
\end{equation}
The word sums already include the direction of the final gradient;
there is no extra dimension factor growing with $n$.
With $w_n=\rho^n/(n!)^2$, the exact identity is
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:transport-weight}
 \frac{w_{j+l}\binom{j+l}{l}}{w_l(j+1)w_{j+1}}
       =\rho^{-1}\frac{j+1}{\binom{j+l}{l}}
       \le\rho^{-1}.
\end{equation}
Here $\binom{j+l}{l}\ge j+1$ because $l\ge1$. Also
$j+1=n-l+1\le n$. Thus every derivative on the right of
\eqref{ipm:expanded-correction-wordcount} is already present when
$n\le M$.

Define, pointwise in time,
\[
 X_M=\sum_{n=0}^M w_n|e|_n,\qquad
 Y_M=\sum_{n=0}^M n w_n|e|_n.
\]
Summing \eqref{ipm:expanded-correction-wordcount}, using
\eqref{ipm:transport-weight}, gives
$C_s\rho^{-1}\|b\|_{\rho,M}Y_M$.
The fixed-base commutators give
$C_s\|b\|_{H^s}X_M$ after dividing each energy inequality by
the corresponding norm. The zero-order and source terms are bounded
by \eqref{ipm:expanded-correction-operator}. Since
\[
 \|b\|_{\rho,M}\le B_0+\Lambda X_M,
 \qquad w_n'=(\rho'/\rho)n w_n,
\]
enlarging the fixed power $\Lambda$ once gives
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:finite-cutoff-energy}
 X_M'\le\Lambda(X_M+X_M^2+R_k)
 +\left[\frac{\rho'}{\rho}
       +\Lambda\rho^{-1}(B_0+X_M)\right]Y_M.
\end{equation}
This is an upper Dini derivative inequality at zeros of individual
norms. To justify it there, regularize each of the finitely many
norms by $(\|v\|_{H^s}^2+\varepsilon^2)^{1/2}$ and let
$\varepsilon\downarrow0$ with $M$ fixed. No error is retained in
the initial norm.

For $M=0$, $Y_M=0$ and there are no additional-word commutators.
For $M=1$, the only such product is
$\partial b\cdot\nabla e$, bounded by $|b|_1|e|_1$; it does
not involve $|e|_2$. These endpoint cases make explicit that the
estimate is not an infinite-order cancellation requiring a nonexistent
top derivative.

\par\smallskip\noindent\textbf{Step 4. Quantitative radius and norm bootstraps.}
Put $\Delta_k=e^{-k^\vartheta/2}$ and choose
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-correction-radius}
 \rho(t)=\rho_0-2\Lambda(B_0+\Delta_k)t.
\end{equation}
The constants determining $\rho_0^{-1}$ and $\Lambda$ are fixed
powers of $\mathcal P$. Choose $Q$ so large that
\eqref{ipm:correction-frequency-separation} implies
\[
 \Lambda\rho_0^{-1}\le k^{\vartheta/100},
 \qquad \Lambda S\le k^{\vartheta/100}.
\]
Since $S\le1$ and $\vartheta$ is a fixed small number, increasing
the universal lower threshold on $k$ then gives
$2\Lambda S(B_0+\Delta_k)\le\rho_0/2$. In particular
$\rho(t)\ge\rho_0/2$.
On a bootstrap interval where $X_M\le\Delta_k\le1$,
the bracket in \eqref{ipm:finite-cutoff-energy} is nonpositive.
Starting from zero and integrating the remaining scalar inequality,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:expanded-correction-bootstrap}
 X_M(t)\le \Lambda S e^{2\Lambda S}R_k<\Delta_k/4.
\end{equation}
To check the strict inequality, take logarithms: the left side has
logarithm at most
$\log(\Lambda S)+2\Lambda S-0.6k^\vartheta\log k$,
which is less than $-k^\vartheta/2-\log4$ for all sufficiently
large $k$. The constants are uniform in $M$.
Continuity closes this bootstrap on the whole interval where the
smooth approximation exists.

\par\smallskip\noindent\textbf{Step 5. Existence without assuming a Gevrey solution.}
Let $\mathcal J_J$ be the orthogonal Fourier projection onto a finite
symmetric box of frequencies of the lifted torus. Include the zero
frequency and choose the boxes nested and invariant under reflection.
Solve
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:galerkin-equation}
 \begin{split}
 (e_J)_t+\mathcal J_J\bigl[
 (b^a+T_{k^{-1}} e_J)\cdot\nabla e_J+Z(e_J)\bigr]
       &=-\mathcal J_J r^a,\\
 e_J(0)&=0,\qquad \mathcal J_J e_J=e_J.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
This is a finite-dimensional ODE with smooth time coefficients and
a locally Lipschitz right side. The velocity $\mathcal V_D e_J$ is
computed by the exact Darcy operator, so its lifted divergence is
zero. It is not necessary to truncate that velocity.

When a differentiated equation is paired with a derivative of $e_J$,
move $\mathcal J_J$ to the test function. The projection commutes
with derivatives and fixes that test function; it therefore disappears
from the energy identity. All the preceding estimates hold uniformly
in $J,M$. The projection contracts every derivative $H^s$ norm.

For a fixed $J$, first use $M=0$. Estimate
\eqref{ipm:expanded-correction-bootstrap} bounds the solution's
$H^s$ norm, and hence all its finitely many Fourier coordinates,
up to time $S$. Finite-dimensional continuation therefore gives
existence on $[0,S]$. Only after this is established do we apply
the same argument for each $M\ge1$. This yields uniform bounds at
every fixed Sobolev order on an already common interval.

For each fixed integer $q$, choose a sufficiently large $M$.
The bounds give $e_J$ bounded uniformly in $C_tH^{q+2}$.
The equation, the fixed-order boundedness of $\mathcal V_D$, and
Sobolev products give a uniform bound for $(e_J)_t$ in $C_tH^q$.
At this stage the torus is fixed; compactness requires no quantitative
constant uniform over different layers. Each fixed Fourier box has
equicontinuous bounded coefficients. A diagonal subsequence converges
uniformly in time on every such box, and the two-derivative bound
controls the complementary Fourier tails. Consequently
\[
 e_J\longrightarrow e
       \quad\hbox{in }C([0,S];H^q)
       \quad\hbox{for every fixed }q.
\]
The inverse $\mathcal V_D(t)$ is uniformly bounded at each such order,
so $\mathcal V_D e_J$ converges there too. Products converge in lower
fixed norms, and $\mathcal J_J$ tends strongly to the identity;
the uniformly higher derivative bounds control its tails even on
these varying products. Passing to \eqref{ipm:galerkin-equation}
gives \eqref{ipm:correction-equation} with $e(0)=0$.

At each finite cutoff the norm estimate passes to the limit.
Monotone convergence in $M$, followed by
$\rho(t)\ge\rho_0/2$, yields
$\sup_t\|e(t)\|_{\rho_0/2}\le\Delta_k$.
The equation gives a continuous first time derivative in every
fixed Sobolev space. Further time derivatives follow inductively:
the coefficients are smooth in time, and time differentiation of
the coercive Darcy equation gives its inverse's differentiated
operator formula at every fixed order. No quantitative bound on
arbitrarily many time derivatives is required for the induction.
Thus $e$ is smooth up to both endpoints of the finite interval.

\par\smallskip\noindent\textbf{Step 6. Derivative bounds and parity.}
At $\rho_*=\rho_0/4$, one derivative costs at most
$C\rho_0^{-1}$ by \eqref{ipm:radiusderivative}.
The bounds on $b,Z,r^a$ and the equation now give
$\|e_t\|_{\rho_*}\le\mathcal P^C\Delta_k$.
The Darcy estimate gives the same bound for $\mathcal V_D e$.
This proves \eqref{ipm:correction-bound}.

All coefficients of the Darcy law are real, and the projections
preserve real functions. For the indicated joint reflection,
the scalar correction equation preserves oddness: its transport
field is odd, its scalar gradient is even, and its residual is odd.
The reflection-invariant Galerkin equations have unique solutions,
so each $e_J$ is real and odd. Both properties pass to the limit.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{ipm:physical-reconstruction}
Under the integer-carrier condition \eqref{ipm:carrier-lattice}, define
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:physical-increments}
 \Theta(t,X(t,\ell y))=\ell k^{-1} A_{\rm diag}(t,y),
 \qquad
 U(t,X(t,\ell y))=\ell k^{-1} F(t,y)v_{\rm diag}(t,y),
\end{equation}
where $f_{\rm diag}(t,y):=f(t,y,km_0\cdot y)$.
Then $P_{\rm new}=P+\Theta$ is an exact odd mean-zero solution with a
smooth periodic pressure $p_{\rm new}$, and
$U=u(P_{\rm new})-u(P)$. Set $\pi=p_{\rm new}-p$.
For the leading fields in \eqref{ipm:intro-leading-matrices}, there
are global physical errors and a fixed exponent $C_*>0$ such that,
with all compositions evaluated at $X(t,\ell y)$,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:packet-gradient-errors}
 \begin{aligned}
 (\nabla\Theta)\circ X
 &=(\nabla\Theta)_{\rm lead}\circ X+E_r\circ X,\\
 (\nabla U)\circ X
 &=(\nabla U)_{\rm lead}\circ X+E_M\circ X,\\
 (\nabla^2\pi)\circ X
 &=(\nabla^2\pi)_{\rm lead}\circ X+E_H\circ X,\\
 \sup_t(\|E_r\|_\infty+\|E_M\|_\infty+\|E_H\|_\infty)
 &\le k^{-1}\mathcal P^{C_*}.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
The chain rule for graph evaluation is
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:diagonal-chain-rule}
 \nabla_y f_{\rm diag}
 =(\nabla_y f+km_0\partial_\theta f)_{\rm diag}
 =k(Df)_{\rm diag}.
\end{equation}
The lattice condition makes this a periodic function on
$\mathbb T_\ell^2$: translating $y$ by a period changes the
angle by an integer multiple of $2\pi$.

\textbf{Step 1. The vector pressure has a genuine periodic potential.}
A lifted Fourier mode has slow frequency $\ell\nu$, with
$\nu\in\mathbb Z^2$, and angular frequency $j\in\mathbb Z$.
Its $D$-frequency is
\[
 \eta=k^{-1}\ell\nu+jm_0
       =\frac{\ell}{k}(\nu+jn_0),\qquad
 n_0=(k/\ell)m_0\in\mathbb Z^2.
\]
If $\eta\ne0$, then $|\eta|\ge\ell/k$. A vector in the closed
$D$-gradient space has its Fourier coefficient parallel to $\eta$
and has zero coefficient at each resonant frequency $\eta=0$.
For the smooth vector pressure $p$ in the Darcy construction define
\[
 \widehat\zeta(\nu,j)=
       \frac{\eta\cdot\widehat p(\nu,j)}{i|\eta|^2}
       \quad(\eta\ne0),\qquad
 \widehat\zeta(\nu,j)=0\quad(\eta=0).
\]
Then $D\zeta=p$ and
$|\widehat\zeta|\le(k/\ell)|\widehat p|$. Thus $\zeta$ is a smooth
periodic potential. Its possible $k/\ell$ norm cost is used only
to establish existence of a smooth potential; all quantitative
operator estimates concerned the vector $p$.

\textbf{Step 2. Darcy law, divergence, and the scalar equation.}
For the increments \eqref{ipm:physical-increments},
let $\pi(t,X(t,\ell y))=\ell^2k^{-2}\zeta_{\rm diag}(t,y)$.
By \eqref{ipm:diagonal-chain-rule},
\[
 (\nabla_x\pi)\circ X(t,\ell y)
       =\ell k^{-1} F^{-T}p_{\rm diag}.
\]
Since $FG=F^{-T}$ and $Fa_2=e_2$, the exact lifted Darcy law gives
$U=-c\Theta e_2-\nabla_x\pi$. This pressure is periodic, because
both the graph function and the inverse parent torus map are periodic
in the required sense.

For divergence, Piola's identity with $\det F=1$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:physical-piola}
 (\operatorname{div}_xU)\circ X
   =\operatorname{div}_z(F^{-1}U\circ X)
   =k^{-1}\operatorname{div}_y v_{\rm diag}
   =(\operatorname{div}_D v)_{\rm diag}=0.
\end{equation}
In the second equality $z=\ell y$ and $F^{-1}U\circ X=\ell k^{-1} v_{\rm diag}$.
Along the parent map,
$(D_t^P\Theta)\circ X=\ell k^{-1}(A_t)_{\rm diag}$ and
$F^T(\nabla P)\circ X=R_0$. Therefore
\[
 \begin{split}
 \bigl[\partial_t(P+\Theta)+(u(P)+U)\cdot\nabla(P+\Theta)\bigr]
                 \circ X(t,\ell y)
 &=\ell k^{-1}\bigl[A_t+v\cdot R_0+v\cdot DA\bigr]_{\rm diag}\\
 &=0.
 \end{split}
\]
This verifies every term and scale factor in the physical equation.

\textbf{Step 3. The physical zero mode.}
Under the joint reflection $(y,\theta)\mapsto(-y,-\theta)$,
$F,G,a_2,R_0,L,b_0,c_A^{\ell}$ are even. The leading $A_1$ is odd.
The mean inverse returns an odd scalar for odd data; the centered
scalar inverse preserves the same parity. In the recurrence the
scalar and velocity coefficients $A_p,V_p,C_p$ are odd under this
joint reflection, while their angular primitives $\pi_p,\psi_p$
are even. Spatial or angular differentiation reverses the joint
parity, which checks the recurrence one term at a time. This joint
parity is distinct from the order-dependent parity under reflection
of $\theta$ alone established in Lemma~\ref{ipm:phase-parity}.

The exact correction is odd by its construction. The physical graph
and the odd parent map carry joint oddness to spatial oddness.
Consequently $\Theta$ and $P+\Theta$ have zero spatial integral.
Integrating the periodic Darcy law now gives $\int U=0$.
Thus the physical velocity is exactly the normalized Darcy velocity.
No zero-mean condition is imposed on the other auxiliary phase leaves.

\textbf{Step 4. Global first-derivative and Hessian errors.}
At a fixed smaller radius, the hierarchy and correction give
\[
 A=A_1+\mathcal R_A,\qquad
 \mathcal V_D A=V_1+\mathcal R_V,\qquad
 \|\mathcal R_A\|_\rho+\|\mathcal R_V\|_\rho
                       \le k^{-1}\mathcal P^C.
\]
The exponentially small correction is included in these inequalities.
One fixed radius decrease supplies the same bound for any fixed
number of lifted derivatives, with a larger fixed polynomial.
Physical differentiation of the scalar in
\eqref{ipm:physical-increments} gives
\[
 (\nabla_x\Theta)\circ X=F^{-T}(DA)_{\rm diag}.
\]
The fast part of $DA_1$ is $m_0\partial_\theta A_1$; its slow
part is $k^{-1}\nabla_yA_1$. Hence
$(\nabla\Theta)\circ X
=m(\partial_\theta A_1)_{\rm diag}+O(k^{-1}\mathcal P^C)$.
For the velocity, differentiate $Fv$ on the graph. Derivatives of
$F$ and other slow coefficients acquire a factor $k^{-1}$, whereas
the leading fast derivative is
\[
 (Fb_0)\otimes(F^{-T}m_0)\,(\partial_\theta A_1)_{\rm diag}
       =\mathfrak m(m)\otimes m\,(\partial_\theta A_1)_{\rm diag}.
\]
Here $Fb_0=\mathfrak m(F^{-T}m_0)$ follows directly by substituting
$G=F^{-1}F^{-T}$ into $L$. All remaining terms are
$O(k^{-1}\mathcal P^C)$ globally.
Finally,
\[
 \nabla^2\pi=-\nabla U-ce_2(\nabla\Theta)^T,\qquad
 \mathfrak m(m)+ce_2=c\,m_2m/|m|^2.
\]
This yields exactly \eqref{ipm:packet-gradient-errors}.
The estimates are global because the exact Darcy projection and
nonlinear correction have been estimated globally. Only the leading
terms are localized.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{ipm:parent-class-return}
For the child of Proposition~\ref{ipm:physical-reconstruction}, let
$\Phi$ be the flow of $b=(k^{-1}v,m_0\cdot v)$ with
$\Phi(0)=\mathrm{id}$, and let $\pi_y$ project onto the first two components.
Define
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:perturbed-label-flow}
 Y(t,z)=\ell\,\pi_y
       \Phi(t,z/\ell,(k/\ell)m_0\cdot z).
\end{equation}
Then $Y$ is volume preserving and $X_{\rm new}=X\circ Y$ is the
actual initial-label flow. The initial density and $X_{\rm new}$,
together with its first two time derivatives, satisfy
\eqref{ipm:parent-class} with $K_{\rm new}\le(K\mathcal P k/\ell)^{C_*}$
for one fixed exponent $C_*$.
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
It remains to show that the exact child provides the hypotheses for
the next layer, with one stage-independent exponent.

\textbf{Step 1. Small lifted transport and its time derivative.}
Use the sharper bound $\|b^a\|_{\rho_0}\le k^{-1}\mathcal P^C$
from the truncated hierarchy, before replacing it by $B_0$ in the
energy estimate. The uniform Sobolev embedding constant is fixed.
Frequency separation therefore gives
$C_sk^{-1}\mathcal P^C\le k^{-1/2}$. The exact correction contributes
at most $C_s\mathcal P^C e^{-k^\vartheta/2}\le k^{-1/2}$.
Together with the Darcy bounds these estimates give
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:small-lifted-transport}
 \sup_t\sum_{|I|=n}\|\partial^Ib(t)\|_\infty
       \le B R_b^n(n!)^2,\qquad
 B\le2k^{-1/2},\quad R_b\le\mathcal P^C.
\end{equation}
The scalar $A=A^a+e$ and its time derivative have polynomial Gevrey
norms at one fixed smaller radius. Differentiating the exact Darcy
equation, as in \eqref{ipm:darcy-time-derivative}, uses only
$G_t,(a_2)_t,A,A_t$. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:lifted-time-coefficient}
 \sup_t\sum_{|I|=n}\|\partial^Ib_t(t)\|_\infty
       \le\mathcal P^C(\mathcal P^C)^n(n!)^2.
\end{equation}
No second scalar time derivative or time differentiation of the
formal pressure arrays is used.

Frequency separation implies $SBR_b$ is smaller than the fixed
threshold in Lemma~\ref{ipm:smallflow}. Thus \eqref{ipm:smallflowbound} gives
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:lifted-flow-estimate}
 \sup_t\sum_{|I|=n}
       \|\partial^I(\Phi-\mathrm{id})\|_\infty
       \le CBS(CR_b)^n(n!)^2.
\end{equation}
That lemma proves this for finite word cutoffs using a positive
composition majorant, then passes to all orders. Equations
$\Phi_t=b\circ\Phi$ and
$\Phi_{tt}=(b_t+(\nabla_{y,\theta}b)b)\circ\Phi$ give fixed polynomial size and
growth constants for the first two time derivatives as well, by
\eqref{ipm:smallflow-first-time}--\eqref{ipm:smallflow-second-time}.
All derivatives here are ordinary derivatives in the lifted
variables, not the degenerate derivative $D$.

\textbf{Step 2. The physical relabeling and its Jacobian.}
For the relabeling \eqref{ipm:perturbed-label-flow}, since $b=(k^{-1} v,m_0\cdot v)$, the quantity
$\theta-km_0\cdot y$ is constant along its trajectories.
Starting on the zero-offset graph therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:label-velocity}
 Y_t(t,z)=\ell k^{-1}
       v(t,Y(t,z)/\ell,km_0\cdot Y(t,z)/\ell).
\end{equation}
The induced vector field has zero divergence in $z$, because
its divergence is $(\operatorname{div}_D v)_{\rm diag}=0$ after
the scaling and graph chain rule. Therefore
$\det D_zY=1$ and $Y(0,z)=z$.
Periodicity of the lifted field and the integer carrier imply
$Y(t,z+2\pi n)=Y(t,z)+2\pi n$. Thus $Y$ is a smooth torus
flow, not only a formal graph map.

Define
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:new-physical-flow}
 X_{\rm new}(t,z)=X(t,Y(t,z)).
\end{equation}
Differentiating in time gives
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:child-flow-identity}
 (X_{\rm new})_t
       =X_t(t,Y)+D_zX(t,Y)Y_t
       =(u(P)+U)(t,X_{\rm new}).
\end{equation}
The last identity uses exactly \eqref{ipm:physical-increments}.
Together with $X_{\rm new}(0,z)=z$, it identifies the map as the
actual initial-label flow of the constructed child.

\textbf{Step 3. Quantitative closure of all four fields.}
Graph differentiation in \eqref{ipm:perturbed-label-flow} multiplies
the derivative growth constant by at most $Ck/\ell$.
The factor $\ell$ outside the flow and the bounds for $\Phi$,
$\Phi_t,\Phi_{tt}$ show that $Y-\mathrm{id},Y_t,Y_{tt}$ have
Gevrey size at most $\mathcal P^C$ and growth at most
$(k/\ell)\mathcal P^C$.
Apply Lemma~\ref{ipm:composition} to
\[
 X_{\rm new}-\mathrm{id}
       =(X-\mathrm{id})\circ Y+(Y-\mathrm{id}).
\]
This expression uses periodic displacement fields, so their zeroth
norms are finite even for a lift of the torus flow.
The composition growth is a fixed polynomial in $K,\mathcal P,k/\ell$.
For the time derivatives use the explicit identities
\[
 \begin{split}
 (X\circ Y)_t
       &=X_t\circ Y+(DX\circ Y)Y_t,\\
 (X\circ Y)_{tt}
       &=X_{tt}\circ Y+2(DX_t\circ Y)Y_t\\
       &\quad +(D^2X\circ Y)[Y_t,Y_t]+(DX\circ Y)Y_{tt}.
 \end{split}
\]
Only the four fields in \eqref{ipm:parent-class} and at most two
additional spatial derivatives appear. A fixed derivative shift
changes their Gevrey size and growth by fixed polynomial factors.
Products and the composition lemma therefore give the same kind
of bound for both time derivatives of $X_{\rm new}$.

At time zero the physical labels are the physical coordinates.
The explicitly prescribed density is $P(0)$ plus the graph of
the finite hierarchy, with no exact correction. Its Gevrey norm
before graph evaluation is polynomial in $\mathcal P$ by the
tail estimate \eqref{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-split-tail}; its graph growth
costs at most $Ck/\ell$.
Hence the initial density satisfies the same fixed polynomial
bound. Choose one exponent $C_*$ larger than all these finitely
many size and growth exponents. Taking
$K_{\rm new}=(K\mathcal P k/\ell)^{C_*}$ then bounds each field's
$n$th derivative by $K_{\rm new}^{n+1}(n!)^2$.
This proves \eqref{ipm:packet-output-class} with exactly the input
class required at the next stage.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- proposition-10: body_md -->
\label{ipm:initial-increment-bounds}
For the exact child of Proposition~\ref{ipm:physical-reconstruction},
the leading density increment is
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:leading-density}
 \Theta_{\rm lead}(t,X(t,\ell y))
 =\frac{\ell}{k}\alpha\chi(y)a(t,y)f_\delta(km_0\cdot y).
\end{equation}
The exact increment decomposes as
$\Theta=\Theta_{\rm osc}+\Theta_{\rm mean}+\Theta_{\rm corr}$, with
finite oscillatory and phase-independent parts and a global exact
correction. For each fixed integer $j\ge0$ and every $t\in[0,S]$,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:packet-history}
 \begin{aligned}
 \|\Theta_{\rm osc}(t)\|_{C^j}
 &\le C_j\ell^{1-j}k^{j-1}\mathcal P^{C_j}\gamma(t),\\
 \|\Theta_{\rm mean}(t)\|_{C^j}
 &\le C_j\ell^{1-j}k^{-2}\mathcal P^{C_j},\\
 \|\Theta_{\rm corr}(t)\|_{C^j}
 &\le C_j\ell^{1-j}k^{j-1}\mathcal P^{C_j}
       e^{-k^\vartheta/2}.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Its oscillatory initial density is exactly $\Theta_{\rm lead}(0)$;
all higher oscillatory coefficients vanish initially, and
$\Theta_{\rm corr}(0)=0$. The remaining initial increment consists
of the phase means selected by Proposition~\ref{ipm:meaninverse}.
<!-- end proposition-10 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
For each fixed $j$, differentiating a graph function $j$ times costs
at most a constant times $k^j$. Scaling $z=\ell y$ costs
$\ell^{-j}$. The identity
$\nabla_x=F^{-T}\nabla_z$, applied recursively, involves only a
fixed number of derivatives of $F^{-1}$ at a fixed $j$.
Their sizes are fixed powers of $K$. Define the physical evaluation
operator by $(\mathcal T_tf)(X(t,\ell y))=\ell k^{-1} f_{\rm diag}(y)$.
The physical scalar prefactor $\ell/k$ gives
\[
 \|\mathcal T_tf\|_{C^j}
       \le C_j\ell^{1-j}k^{j-1}\mathcal P^{C_j}\|f\|_\rho.
\]
This inequality is precisely the iterated chain rule for
\eqref{ipm:physical-increments}.
Sobolev embedding in the lifted variables supplies evaluation on the
graph without an additional frequency factor.

By \eqref{ipm:expanded-hierarchy-split-tail} and the leading coefficient
bound, the finite oscillatory hierarchy has norm at most
$\mathcal P^C\gamma(t)$ after summation; its small factor is retained
before time suprema are taken. The mean density is
$\ell\sum_{p=2}^Nk^{-p}B_p$. It has no angular derivatives and
therefore costs no $k^j$, yielding
$C_j\ell^{1-j}k^{-2}\mathcal P^{C_j}$.
Apply \eqref{ipm:correction-bound} separately to obtain the final
line of \eqref{ipm:packet-history}; no factor $\gamma(t)$ is asserted
for that correction. At time zero, all $A_p$ for $p\ge2$ and $e$
vanish. The only initial oscillation is $A_1(0)$, which gives
\eqref{ipm:leading-density} at $t=0$, and the selected initial means are precisely those included in the mean bound.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- proposition-11: body_md -->
\label{ipm:packet}
There are fixed positive constants $r,Q,C_*$, a fixed integer $h$,
and a fixed sufficiently small $\vartheta>0$ with the following property.
Suppose \eqref{ipm:parent-class} holds, $\nabla^2p\le I$ on $[0,S]$, and
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:packet-separation}
 \ell K^{2A+3}\le\varepsilon_*.
\end{equation}
Assume also the frequency separation \eqref{ipm:correction-frequency-separation}.
Then an exact odd mean-zero unforced solution $P_{\rm new}$ exists on
$[0,S]$. Write $\Theta=P_{\rm new}-P$, $m=F^{-T}m_0$, and evaluate lifted
quantities at $(t,y,\theta)=(t,y,km_0\cdot y)$ when composing with
$x=X(t,\ell y)$. Its leading density increment is given by \eqref{ipm:leading-density}.
The global gradient and Hessian errors satisfy
\eqref{ipm:packet-gradient-errors} of Proposition~\ref{ipm:physical-reconstruction}.
The exact increment has the decomposition and all-time bounds
\eqref{ipm:packet-history} of Proposition~\ref{ipm:initial-increment-bounds}.
The oscillatory initial density is exactly \eqref{ipm:leading-density}
at $t=0$, and $\Theta_{\rm corr}(0)=0$.
Finally, $P_{\rm new}(0)$ and the initial-label flow $X_{\rm new}$,
including its first two time derivatives, satisfy
\eqref{ipm:parent-class} with
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:packet-output-class}
 K_{\rm new}\le(K\mathcal P k/\ell)^{C_*}.
\end{equation}
All exponents are the same at every invocation. The proposition does not
assert $\nabla^2p_{\rm new}\le I$; that conclusion requires the geometric
sign and size estimates for the leading pressure-Hessian increment in \eqref{ipm:intro-leading-matrices}.
<!-- end proposition-11 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Lemma~\ref{ipm:packet-calculus} and Corollary~\ref{ipm:common-growth} supply
the coefficient bounds and the three inverses.
Proposition~\ref{ipm:hierarchy-construction} and
Lemma~\ref{ipm:coefficients} construct and estimate the hierarchy.
Proposition~\ref{ipm:finite-approximation}, Proposition~\ref{ipm:correction},
and Proposition~\ref{ipm:physical-reconstruction} give the exact
physical child and its gradient estimates.
Proposition~\ref{ipm:parent-class-return} recovers the parent class,
and Proposition~\ref{ipm:initial-increment-bounds} supplies the initial data
and all-time increment bounds.
It remains to choose their constants in a common order.
First fix the dimension, $s$, cutoff, and profile constants.
The algebra of the parent class determines $A_0$ and the exponent $A$ in
Lemma~\ref{ipm:tube-localization}; the localization exponent $2A+3$ suffices for
\eqref{ipm:centered-propagator}.
Next derive the polynomial thresholds and prefactors in the mean,
scalar, and lifted Darcy inverses, including
\eqref{ipm:darcy-time-derivative}. This is a finite list independent
of the hierarchy depth. Choose $r$ so $R=\mathcal P^r$ exceeds those
thresholds and the leading profile growth, and then choose $h$ to
cover fixed shift costs. The hierarchy determines the fixed exponents
$A,D$ in \eqref{ipm:order-coefficient-bound} and the low-order sizes in
\eqref{ipm:approximation-bounds}. Choose $\vartheta>0$ with
$D\vartheta<1/200$, as in the truncation estimate.
The exact correction and flow calculation now determine their fixed
polynomial exponents and a fixed $C_*$ dominating the finitely many
outputs of Proposition~\ref{ipm:packet}.
Finally choose $Q$ to enforce all frequency separations used for
truncation, radius retention, Gronwall domination, and small lifted
flow. Increasing $Q$ changes none of the preceding exponents.
Choose the universal threshold $k_*$ last.

All applications of the mean, scalar, and Darcy inverses in the
hierarchy accept and return the same $R$ and arbitrary input shift.
The only radius decrease depending on evolution is the single one
in Proposition~\ref{ipm:correction}, performed after the hierarchy has been
truncated. Its initial and final radii are fixed inverse powers of
$\mathcal P$. No inverse amplitude seed enters any choice. This
completes the proof of Proposition~\ref{ipm:packet}.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{ipm:five-transfer-errors}
Under this setup, the remainders and ray variables satisfy
\[
 |R_r|+\|R_M\|+\|R_H\|+|\alpha'|+|(\log G)'|
 \le C_E(1+h_{\rm old}),\qquad C_E:=5+C_{\nabla}(1+6c).
\]
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Oddness of the flow of $O$ fixes the origin. The angular identity in \eqref{ipm:intro-angular-speed} and the amplitude identity \eqref{ipm:rayidentity} therefore give
\[
 |\alpha'|\le\|M_O\|,\qquad
 |(\log G)'|=|(n^\perp)^TH_On^\perp|\le\|H_O\|.
\]
From $M_O=-H_O-ce_2r_O^T$ and
$\operatorname{tr}H_O=-c(r_O)_2$, the upper bound $H_O\le I$ implies
\[
 \|H_O\|\le1+cC_{\nabla}h_{\rm old},\qquad
 \|M_O\|\le1+2cC_{\nabla}h_{\rm old}.
\]
Adding the five quantities required by Lemma~\ref{ipm:amplification} gives the
fully explicit bound
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:seventh-cascade-five-errors}
 \begin{aligned}
 |R_r|+\|R_M\|+\|R_H\|+|\alpha'|+|(\log G)'|
 &\le |r_O|+2\|M_O\|+2\|H_O\|+\epsilon\\
 &\le4+C_{\nabla}(1+6c)h_{\rm old}+\epsilon\\
 &\le C_E(1+h_{\rm old}).
 \end{aligned}\qedhere
\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{ipm:explicit-packet-budget}
Let a layer over an exact parent $P$ have activation $s$, target
$t_*$, retained horizon $S=t_*+2W_{\rm next}\le1$, target gradient
$h$, and target angle $\pi/2+1/x_{\rm tar}$, where
$x_{\rm tar}\ge x\ge4$. Suppose its angle at the origin increases
on $[s,t_*]$ from at least $1/(2x)$. Set
$g(t)=\alpha a(t,0)|m(t,0)|/\delta$, so $g(t_*)=h$, and assume
\eqref{ipm:cascade-future-localization} and
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:seventh-cascade-target-gain}
 \frac{g(t_*)}{g(s)}\ge e^{c_0x},\qquad
 H(P)\le I,\qquad \|\nabla P\|_\infty\le C_{\nabla}h_{\rm par}.
\end{equation}
Then the retained-angle bounds proved in
Proposition~\ref{ipm:packet-history-geometry} hold, and the three
time-range bounds and positive supported phase satisfy
\eqref{ipm:seventh-cascade-three-time-ranges}--\eqref{ipm:seventh-cascade-supported-positive}.
For the cutoff and profile of Lemma~\ref{ipm:profilelemma}, with
the constant $C_f$ in \eqref{ipm:profile-bounds}, the global pressure and gradient estimates
\eqref{ipm:seventh-cascade-global-budget}--\eqref{ipm:seventh-cascade-gradient-bounds}
hold with the explicit constants displayed there. If
$|m(t,0)|^{-1}\le B_mK^A$, then the seed bounds
\eqref{ipm:seventh-cascade-seed-parameter} also hold.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
The first condition in \eqref{ipm:seventh-cascade-target-gain} is supplied by
\eqref{ipm:transfer-gain} in Lemma~\ref{ipm:amplification};
the other two are inherited exact-parent bounds.

The first bound in \eqref{ipm:cascade-future-localization} gives
$|\beta(t)-\beta(t_*)|\le1/(4x_{\rm tar})$ for $t_*\le t\le S$,
as in the proof of Proposition~\ref{ipm:packet-history-geometry}.
Moreover, with $n(t)=m(t,0)/|m(t,0)|$,
\[
 \frac d{dt}\log g=-(n^\perp)^TH(P)n^\perp
 \le1+cC_{\nabla}h_{\rm par}.
\]
Thus \eqref{ipm:cascade-future-localization} bounds its forward
growth exponent by $1/(4x_{\rm tar})$, and in particular
$g(t)\le2h$ for $t_*\le t\le S$.
The lower logarithmic derivative bound $-1$ gives the two backward
bounds
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:seventh-cascade-three-time-ranges}
 \begin{aligned}
 g(t)&\le e h e^{-c_0x},&&0\le t\le s,\\
 g(t)&\le e h,&&s\le t\le t_*,\\
 g(t)&\le2h,&&t_*\le t\le S.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here $S\le1$ was used to replace each backward factor by $e$.
The first line uses the gain in
\eqref{ipm:seventh-cascade-target-gain}; it is not a forward
stability assertion.

The second bound in \eqref{ipm:cascade-future-localization} ensures
$\ell K^{2A+3}\le1/(20\pi)$. Hence
\eqref{ipm:cascade-tube-comparison} gives, for
$y\in\operatorname{supp}\chi$ and $t\in[0,S]$,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:seventh-cascade-tube-hypothesis}
 \frac{a(t,\ell y)|m(t,\ell y)|}{a(t,0)|m(t,0)|}\le2,
 \qquad
 \left|\frac{m(t,\ell y)}{|m(t,\ell y)|}-n(t)\right|
 \le\frac1{10\pi x_{\rm tar}}.
\end{equation}

On the active interval $[s,t_*]$, the angle range \eqref{ipm:transfer-angle-range} in Lemma~\ref{ipm:amplification} gives
\[
 \sin\beta(t)\ge\sin(1/(2x))\ge\frac1{\pi x},\qquad s\le t\le t_*.
\]
The direction error in
\eqref{ipm:seventh-cascade-tube-hypothesis} is at most
$1/(10\pi x)$, so the second phase component on the entire active
supported tube is positive. On the retained interval, the angle estimate following from
\eqref{ipm:cascade-future-localization}, as in
Proposition~\ref{ipm:packet-history-geometry}, gives
$\sin\beta(t)\ge1/2$, with an even smaller relative direction error. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:seventh-cascade-supported-positive}
 m_2(t,\ell y)>0\quad\hbox{on the supported tube for }s\le t\le S.
\end{equation}
No assertion about the sign of this component is made on $[0,s]$.

Use $C_\chi$ from Corollary~\ref{ipm:packet-budget} and the profile bounds
\eqref{ipm:profile-bounds}.
Use the leading pressure-Hessian increment in \eqref{ipm:intro-leading-matrices},
evaluated at $X(t,\ell y)$.
For a unit vector $v$, on $[s,S]$ the sign just proved gives
\[
 v^T(\nabla^2\pi)_{\rm lead}v
 \le cC_f\alpha\chi a|m|
 \le2e c C_\chi C_f\delta h.
\]
On $[0,s]$ we instead use its absolute value and the first line of
\eqref{ipm:seventh-cascade-three-time-ranges}:
\[
 \|(\nabla^2\pi)_{\rm lead}\|
 \le cC_f^2\frac{\alpha\chi a|m|}{\delta}
 \le2e c C_\chi C_f^2h e^{-c_0x}.
\]
Let the sum of the three global layer remainder norms be at most
$\epsilon$. The time split and the fact that the leading pressure-Hessian
increment vanishes outside the tube give, globally,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:seventh-cascade-global-budget}
 H(P+\Theta)\le H(P)
 +\left[C_H(\delta h+h e^{-c_0x})+\epsilon\right]I,
 \qquad C_H=2e c C_\chi(C_f+C_f^2).
\end{equation}
In particular a global phase mean is covered by the global remainder;
it has not been assigned the oscillatory history factor.

With $C_{\rm lead}$ defined in \eqref{ipm:cascade-gradient-increment},
the same estimates give the two gradient bounds used in the induction
and limit:
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:seventh-cascade-gradient-bounds}
 \begin{aligned}
 \|\nabla\Theta(t)\|_\infty
 &\le C_{\rm lead}h+\epsilon,
       &&0\le t\le S,\\
 \|\nabla\Theta(t)\|_\infty+
       \|\nabla u(\Theta)(t)\|_\infty
 &\le(1+c)C_{\rm lead}h e^{-c_0x}+\epsilon,
       &&0\le t\le s.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The velocity bound uses $|\mathfrak m(\xi)|\le c$ and the global
velocity-gradient remainder. These constants are fixed before
choosing the scales.

Finally, if the parent class gives
$|m(t,0)|^{-1}\le B_mK^A$, then
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:seventh-cascade-seed-parameter}
 \sup_{[0,S]}\gamma(t)\le eB_mK^A\delta h,
 \qquad
 \gamma(t)\le eB_mK^A\delta h e^{-c_0x}\quad(t\le s).
\end{equation}
This follows from $\gamma=\delta g/|m|$. Neither formula requires a
lower bound for $\alpha$ or for $\gamma$.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{ipm:scale-envelopes}
Put $X=x_{J-1}\ge1$ and $a_j=((j-1)!/(J-1)!)^2$.
For fixed $p,r,q\ge0$, $b>0$, and $J\ge2q+4$, the sequence
$x_{j-1}=Xa_j$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \frac{a_{j+1}/(j+1)^q}{a_j/j^q}
       =j^2\left(\frac j{j+1}\right)^q\ge2,\qquad j\ge J.
 \label{ipm:expanded-cascade-doubling}
\end{equation}
For a finite constant $D_{p,r,q,J}$ independent of $X$,
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{j\ge J}
 \frac{p\log j+r\log(Xa_j)}{Xa_j/j^q}
       \le D_{p,r,q,J}\frac{1+\log X}{X}
       \longrightarrow0\quad(X\to\infty).
 \label{ipm:expanded-cascade-polynomial-uniformity}
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\[
 \sum_{j\ge J}j^p x_{j-1}^r e^{-b x_{j-1}/j^q}
       \longrightarrow0\qquad(X\to\infty).
\]
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
The definition of $a_j$ gives
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-doubling}, since $J\ge2q+4$.
Thus $x_{j-1}/j^q\ge (X/J^q)2^{j-J}$.
To prove \eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-polynomial-uniformity}, note that
$\log a_j\le2j\log j$ and $a_j\ge J^{2(j-J)}$.
Hence $j^q(p\log j+r\log a_j)/a_j$ is bounded. The coefficient
of $\log X$ is bounded by $rJ^q2^{-(j-J)}$ using
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-doubling}.
Consequently, for fixed $b>0$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j\ge J}j^p x_{j-1}^r e^{-b x_{j-1}/j^q}
       \longrightarrow0\quad(X\to\infty).
 \label{ipm:expanded-cascade-uniform-series}
\end{equation}
To prove the summation assertion, use
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-polynomial-uniformity} to absorb the
polynomial by half the exponent. With $A=bX/(2J^q)$, the remaining
series is bounded by
$\sum_{n\ge0}e^{-A2^n}\le e^{-A}/(1-e^{-A})$.
Only $q\in\{0,2,3,7/2,5,7\}$ is needed for the construction.
The polynomial degrees used later for a fixed norm can be arbitrary
without changing this finite list or the chosen $J$.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{ipm:scales}
After choosing $J$ sufficiently large and then $x_{J-1}$ sufficiently
large, the following inequalities hold at every stage. Suppose
$K_{j-1}\le k_{j-1}^{A_K}$, and choose $\Gamma_j$ by
\eqref{ipm:cascade-gamma-choice}. Then
\begin{align}
 x_j\ell_jK_{j-1}^{2A+3}&\ll1,&
 \mathcal P_j^Q&\le k_j^{\vartheta/100},
       \label{ipm:cascade-scale-interface}\\
 k_j^{-1}\mathcal P_j^{C_{\rm out}}&\le k_j^{-1/2},&
 (K_{j-1}\mathcal P_j k_j/\ell_j)^{C_{\rm out}}
       &\le k_j^{A_K}.
       \label{ipm:cascade-scale-output}
\end{align}
For $j>J$, the geometric errors $E_j\le C(1+h_{j-2})$ satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \frac{E_jj^2x_{j-1}^3}{h_{j-1}}\ll1.
 \label{ipm:cascade-scale-geometry}
\end{equation}
Furthermore,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{W_{j+1}}{W_j}\le\frac1{16},\qquad
 (1+h_{j-1})W_{j+1}\ll x_j^{-1},
 \label{ipm:cascade-scale-horizons}
\end{equation}
and each of the series
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j\ge J}\delta_jh_j,\qquad
 \sum_{j\ge J}h_je^{-c_0x_{j-1}},\qquad
 \sum_{j\ge J}k_j^{-1/2}
 \label{ipm:cascade-budget-series}
\end{equation}
can be made arbitrarily small by increasing the base size.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Write $x=x_{j-1}$. For $j>J$, the definitions give
\begin{equation}
 \log h_{j-1}=\frac{x}{(j-1)^7},\qquad
 \log h_{j-2}=\frac{x}{(j-1)^2(j-2)^7},\qquad
 \log\widehat k_{j-1}=\frac{x}{(j-1)^4}.
 \label{ipm:cascade-prior-scales}
\end{equation}
The first and third formulas hold at $j=J$ by
\eqref{ipm:cascade-base-bookkeeping}; the second at $j=J+1$
uses the prescribed base value and is also exact.
To handle rounding explicitly, the mean-value theorem gives
$|\log k_j-\log\widehat k_j|
\le2C\ell_j/\widehat k_j$ once $C\ell_j/\widehat k_j\le1/2$.
Thus, for the fixed starting index $J$, one sufficiently large base size
$x_{J-1}$ ensures, uniformly in $j\ge J$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac9{10}\frac{x}{j^2}\le\log k_j
       \le\frac{11}{10}\frac{x}{j^2},\qquad
 \log K_{j-1}\le\frac{11A_K}{10}\frac{x}{(j-1)^4}.
 \label{ipm:expanded-cascade-rounded-logs}
\end{equation}
The second bound uses the inherited hypothesis, with its exact
bookkeeping version at the first step.

For a sufficiently large fixed $J$, denominator comparison gives
\[
 \log(h_j/h_{j-1})\ge\frac{x}{2j^5},\qquad
 \log(h_{j-1}/h_{j-2})\ge\frac{x}{2j^7}\quad(j>J).
\]
For example, the second difference equals
$x(j-1)^{-7}[1-(j-1)^5/(j-2)^7]$, whose bracket is at least
$1/2$ when $J$ is large. Since $h_{j-2}\ge1$, the geometric error
in \eqref{ipm:cascade-scale-geometry} is at most
$2C_Ej^2x^3e^{-x/(2j^7)}$. Formula
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-uniform-series} makes its supremum
smaller than the prescribed transfer threshold. The first step has
exactly frozen geometry and no such error.

For the horizons use
\[
 \frac{W_{j+1}}{W_j}=j^4\frac{h_{j-1}}{h_j},\qquad
 (1+h_{j-1})W_{j+1}x_j
       \le C j^6x^3\frac{1+h_{j-1}}{h_j}.
\]
Both right sides are bounded by fixed polynomials times
$e^{-x/(2j^5)}$. Their uniform smallness gives the precise factor
$1/16$ and the chosen future-angle threshold. It also gives
$h_{j-1}/h_j\le1/4$, as required for the global gradient induction.

The localization logarithm satisfies
\[
 \log(x_j\ell_jK_{j-1}^{2A+3})
   \le \log(j^2x)-\frac{x}{j^{7/2}}
                   +\frac{2(2A+3)A_Kx}{(j-1)^4}.
\]
Choose $J$ so large that the last term is at most
$x/(2j^{7/2})$. Formula
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-polynomial-uniformity} then makes the
entire expression arbitrarily negative, proving both the analytic
and the stronger supported-direction localization.

From $\Gamma_j=\max(1,CK_{j-1}^A\delta_jh_j)$,
$\log\Gamma_j\le C+A\log K_{j-1}+\log h_j$.
For a sum of five positive terms, its logarithm is at most $\log5$
plus the largest logarithm. Thus
\[
 \log\mathcal P_j
   \le C\left(1+\frac{A_Kx}{(j-1)^4}
                   +\frac{x}{j^3}+\frac{x}{j^5}\right)
   \le D\frac{x}{j^3},
 \qquad
 \frac{\log\mathcal P_j}{\log k_j}\le\frac{2D}{j}.
\]
Choose $D$ using only the fixed constants and $A_K$, before the final
choice of $J$. This is possible, for example, by bounding
$(j/(j-1))^4\le16$ for all $j\ge2$. Terms independent of $x$
are absorbed by increasing the base. The choices
$J\ge200QD/\vartheta$ and $J\ge4C_{\rm out}D$ respectively give
the frequency and remainder bounds in the statement.

The logarithm of the proposed new $K$, divided by $\log k_j$, is
bounded explicitly by
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\log[(K_{j-1}\mathcal P_jk_j/\ell_j)^{C_{\rm out}}]}
             {\log k_j}
 \le C_{\rm out}
       \left(1+\frac{3A_K}{j^2}
                 +\frac{2D}{j}+\frac2{j^{3/2}}\right).
 \label{ipm:expanded-cascade-regularity-recurrence}
\end{equation}
Here the first fraction follows from
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-rounded-logs}, after making $J$ larger
than one fixed absolute constant. Choose $J$ so that the three
fractions sum to at most $1/2$. The right side is then
$3C_{\rm out}/2<A_K$, proving the output bound with the very same
$A_K$ at every stage. A fixed power of the previous $K$ is affordable;
a new regularity exponent is not selected at later stages.

The three summands in \eqref{ipm:cascade-budget-series} are bounded by
fixed constants times, respectively,
\[
 e^{-x/(2j^3)},\qquad e^{-c_0x/2},\qquad e^{-x/(3j^2)}.
\]
The inequalities follow respectively from
$j^{-5}\le(2j^3)^{-1}$, $j^{-5}\le c_0/2$, and the lower
rounded carrier bound. Their sums tend to zero by
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-uniform-series}.
There have been finitely many lower bounds on $J$, all involving
constants fixed earlier. Fix such a $J$. All subsequent requirements
are uniform smallness consequences of increasing the single base $X$,
so one base meets them simultaneously. This proves the claimed
order of quantifiers.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{ipm:stationary-base}
For every sufficiently large integer $N$, set
$\nu=(-1,N)$ and $x_{J-1}=1/\arctan(1/N)$, and use the base
levels in \eqref{ipm:cascade-base-bookkeeping}. There is an odd
mean-zero stationary solution $P_{J-1}$ with gradient at the origin
$h_{J-1}\nu/|\nu|$, upper Hessian at most $I/4$, and global
gradient at most $Ch_{J-1}$. Its initial density and explicit
particle map satisfy the four-field class with
$K_{J-1}\le k_{J-1}^{A_K}$. One may take
$t_{J-1}=0$, $S_{J-1}=2W_J\le1$; the first transfer has zero
geometric remainder.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
To realize the base, choose an integer $N$ and put
\begin{equation}
 \nu=(-1,N),\qquad
 x_{J-1}=\frac1{\arctan(1/N)},\qquad n_{\rm b}=\nu/|\nu|.
 \label{ipm:cascade-base-angle}
\end{equation}
The angle of $n_{\rm b}$ is exactly $\pi/2+1/x_{J-1}$, and
$|\nu|\asymp x_{J-1}$ because $\arctan(1/N)\asymp1/N$.
The admissible base sizes tend to infinity as $N$ runs through the
integers. The uniform scale conclusions therefore remain available
with this arithmetic restriction.
Fix a small constant $d_0>0$ such that
$cC_fd_0\le1/4$. With $h=h_{J-1}$ define
\begin{equation}
 \delta_{\rm b}=d_0/h,\qquad b=d_0/|\nu|,\qquad
 P_{J-1}(z)=b f_{\delta_{\rm b}}(\nu\cdot z).
 \label{ipm:cascade-base}
\end{equation}
Its velocity is $b\mathfrak m(\nu)f_{\delta_{\rm b}}(\nu\cdot z)$ and
$\mathfrak m(\nu)\cdot\nu=0$, so this is an exact stationary solution.
Indeed every nonzero Fourier mode of the density is a multiple of
$\nu$, and the degree-zero even symbol has the same value on all
such multiples. Its mean mode is zero. Thus the displayed velocity
is exact, and its dot product with
$b\nu f'_{\delta_{\rm b}}(\nu\cdot z)$ vanishes identically.
It is odd and mean zero, and
\begin{equation}
 \nabla P_{J-1}(0)=h n_{\rm b},\qquad
 H(P_{J-1})=-cb\nu_2\frac{\nu\otimes\nu}{|\nu|^2}
                           f'_{\delta_{\rm b}}(\nu\cdot z)
                  \le\tfrac14 I.
 \label{ipm:cascade-base-hessian}
\end{equation}
By \eqref{ipm:profile-bounds}, its global density gradient satisfies
$\|\nabla P_{J-1}\|_\infty\le b|\nu|C_f^2/\delta_{\rm b}=C_f^2h$.
Its initial-label flow is the explicit shear
\begin{equation}
 X_{J-1}(t,a)=a+t u(P_{J-1})(a),\qquad
 (X_{J-1})_t=u(P_{J-1})(a),\qquad
 (X_{J-1})_{tt}=0.
 \label{ipm:cascade-base-flow}
\end{equation}
Indeed $\nu\cdot X$ is constant along each particle. The rank-one
matrix $D u(P_{J-1})$ squares to zero, whence
$F^{-1}=I-tD u(P_{J-1})$ and $\det F=1$.

We check the entire inherited class, including derivative counts,
rather than only the first few norms. For any ordered word $I$ of
length $n$, differentiating the plane wave multiplies
$f_{\delta_{\rm b}}^{(n)}(\nu\cdot a)$ by a product of $n$ components
of $\nu$. Summing these products in absolute value gives
$(|\nu_1|+|\nu_2|)^n\le(\sqrt2|\nu|)^n$. Consequently
\[
 [P_{J-1}]_n+[u(P_{J-1})]_n
       \le C b\left(\frac{C|\nu|}{\delta_{\rm b}}\right)^n(n!)^2.
\]
The same estimate holds for $X_{J-1}-\operatorname{id}$ on $[0,1]$
and for $(X_{J-1})_t$, while $(X_{J-1})_{tt}=0$.
Taking
$K_{J-1}=C(1+x_{J-1}h/d_0)$ with a sufficiently large fixed $C$
therefore bounds all four fields in exactly the convention of
\eqref{ipm:parent-class}. In particular
\[
 \log K_{J-1}
    \le C+\log x_{J-1}+\frac{x_{J-1}}{(J-1)^7}
    \le A_K\log k_{J-1}
\]
for the already large $J$ and then sufficiently large $N$.
For example the coefficient inequality
$(J-1)^{-7}\le A_K[2(J-1)^4]^{-1}$ holds at all our allowed $J$,
and the remaining $C+\log x_{J-1}$ is absorbed by the other half
as $N\to\infty$. No physical carrier at the bookkeeping scale
$k_{J-1}$ is needed, and no exponential deformation bound for the
flow has been used.

Choose $N$ large enough for all scale requirements and for
$2W_J=4x_{J-1}^2/(ch_{J-1})\le1$.
This is possible since its denominator is
$\exp[x_{J-1}/(J-1)^7]$.
Also $\delta_{\rm b}\le\delta_0$ for such $N$.
Set $t_{J-1}=0$ and $S_{J-1}=2W_J$.
The first transfer has exactly frozen parent geometry and $E=0$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- proposition-12: body_md -->
\label{ipm:ordered-stage-closure}
Under the scale conditions of Lemma~\ref{ipm:scales}, an exact
parent satisfying the induction bounds determines the carrier,
target time, retained horizon, and seed before any child mean is
selected. The resulting horizon lies inside the parent's interval.
With $C_{\nabla}=4\max(C_{\rm lead},C_f^2,1)$, the conditions
$h_{j-1}/h_j\le1/4$, $\epsilon_j\le1$, and $h_j\ge1$ preserve
the global gradient bound. A base upper Hessian $I/4$ and a total
budget at most $1/2$ preserve $H(P_j)\le3I/4$ at every stage.
<!-- end proposition-12 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
The foregoing estimates can be used with the following strictly ordered
choices at stage $j$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item The numerical levels $x_i,h_i,\ell_i,\delta_i,\widehat k_i,W_i$
are prescribed from $J$ and the base size. The parent $P_{j-1}$ is
already known on $[0,t_{j-1}+2W_j]$.
\item Use its known value $F_{j-1}(t_{j-1},0)$ to choose and round the
initial phase vector. Solve its parent-transported phase ODE and
determine the first target time $t_j$. This calculation uses no
coefficient of the new layer.
\item Set $S_j=t_j+2W_{j+1}$. The next width is a prescribed number,
not a function of the child solution. Restrict the known parent to
$[0,S_j]$, normalize the seed at $t_j$, and choose $\Gamma_j$ using
\eqref{ipm:seventh-cascade-seed-parameter}.
\item Solve the finite triangular hierarchy on this fixed interval,
including its terminally selected means at time $S_j$, and then make
the exact scalar correction. These mean initial data may depend on
the known parent's future, but they do not alter that parent or the
already selected $t_j,S_j$.
\item Apply \eqref{ipm:seventh-cascade-global-budget} and
\eqref{ipm:seventh-cascade-gradient-bounds} to the returned exact
child. Its Hessian bound is an output of this step, not an input to
its own mean inverse.
\end{enumerate}
The inequality $W_{j+1}\le W_j/16$ and the transfer bound
\eqref{ipm:transfer-time} give
\[
 S_j\le t_{j-1}+\tfrac34W_j+\tfrac18W_j
     <t_{j-1}+2W_j=S_{j-1}.
\]
Thus the parent data used in every step exist on the required interval.

Using the fixed choice $C_{\nabla}=4\max(C_{\rm lead},C_f^2,1)$, suppose
$h_{j-1}/h_j\le1/4$, $\epsilon_j\le1$, and $h_j\ge1$.
Then the full global gradient induction is the explicit inequality
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:seventh-cascade-gradient-induction}
 \|\nabla P_j\|_\infty
 \le C_{\nabla}h_{j-1}+C_{\rm lead}h_j+\epsilon_j
 \le\tfrac34C_{\nabla}h_j<C_{\nabla}h_j.
\end{equation}
If the base upper Hessian is at most $I/4$ and the sum of the budget
increments in \eqref{ipm:seventh-cascade-global-budget} is at most
$1/2$, every exact stage has $H(P_j)\le3I/4<I$. The constants
$C_{\rm lead},C_H,C_{\nabla},C_E$ have now all been fixed in a finite order.
The scale lemma may consequently choose one $J$ and one base size to
meet their finitely many tolerances. Estimates in higher fixed
$C^m$ norms are used only later to choose convergence tails; they do
not enter this stage closure.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- proposition-13: body_md -->
\label{ipm:stage-induction}
For the fixed scales and stationary base above, there are exact odd
mean-zero solutions $P_j$ on $[0,S_j]$, $j\ge J$, and target
times $t_j$ such that
\[
 \frac14W_j\le t_j-t_{j-1}\le\frac34W_j,
 \qquad S_j=t_j+2W_{j+1}<S_{j-1}.
\]
Every stage satisfies
\[
 H(P_j)\le b_jI\le\tfrac34I,
 \qquad \|\nabla P_j\|_\infty\le C_{\nabla}h_j,
 \qquad K_j\le k_j^{A_K}.
\]
The $j$th leading density-gradient increment at the origin has magnitude
$h_j$ and angle $\pi/2+1/x_j$ at $t_j$. Its carrier satisfies the integer-lattice
condition. Its initial density contains the oscillatory seed and
the selected phase means; its exact residual correction vanishes
at time zero.
<!-- end proposition-13 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Inductively the exact odd stage $P_{j-1}$ is defined on
$[0,S_{j-1}]$, where
\begin{equation}
 S_{j-1}=t_{j-1}+2W_j,\qquad
 H(P_{j-1})\le I,\qquad
 \|\nabla P_{j-1}\|_\infty\le C_{\nabla} h_{j-1},\qquad
 K_{j-1}\le k_{j-1}^{A_K}.
 \label{ipm:cascade-induction}
\end{equation}
For $j>J$, the previous leading density-gradient increment at the origin
has magnitude $h_{j-1}$ and angle $\pi/2+1/x_{j-1}$ at $t_{j-1}$.
Although this leading ray is not transported by $P_{j-1}$, it is
transported by the exact older solution $P_{j-2}$, which is available
on the entire current interval. The phase evolution after
\eqref{ipm:intro-phase}, the identity \eqref{ipm:rayidentity}, and the
matrix bounds \eqref{ipm:matrixbounds} imply
\[
 |(\log G_{j-1})'|+|\alpha_{j-1}'|
       \le C(1+h_{j-2}).
\]
The global layer errors and the older density-gradient bound give
\eqref{ipm:transfer-decomposition}--\eqref{ipm:transfer-error} for
$P_{j-1}$, with
\begin{equation}
 E_j\le C(1+h_{j-2})+C\epsilon_{j-1}
            \le C'(1+h_{j-2}).
 \label{ipm:cascade-error-generation}
\end{equation}
Here are the individual terms in this error estimate. With
$O=P_{j-2}$ and the previous leading density-gradient increment at the origin denoted by
$G_{j-1}n_{j-1}$, linearity of the Darcy operator gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 r_{P_{j-1}}-G_{j-1}n_{j-1}&=r_O+E_r,\\
 M_{P_{j-1}}+cG_{j-1}(n_{j-1})_1
                    n_{j-1}^\perp\otimes n_{j-1}&=M_O+E_M,\\
 H_{P_{j-1}}+cG_{j-1}(n_{j-1})_2
                    n_{j-1}\otimes n_{j-1}&=H_O+E_H.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The three $E$ terms have norm sum at most $\epsilon_{j-1}$.
Furthermore
$\|r_O\|\le C_{\nabla}h_{j-2}$,
$\|M_O\|\le1+2cC_{\nabla}h_{j-2}$, and
$\|H_O\|\le1+cC_{\nabla}h_{j-2}$.
The derivatives of $\alpha_{j-1}$ and $\log G_{j-1}$ are bounded
by the latter two norms because their ray is transported by $O$.
Adding these estimates proves
$E_j\le C_E(1+h_{j-2})$, since $\epsilon_{j-1}\le1$.
Thus the constant $C_E$ used when choosing the scales depends only
on previously fixed constants.
\label{ipm:expanded-cascade-two-generation-error}

Set $s=t_{j-1}$ and select the child phase. Let
$v$ be the unit vector with angle $1/x_{j-1}$, perpendicular to the
previous leading density-gradient increment in the desired orientation. The ideal unit
initial vector is
\[
 m_0^*=\frac{F_{j-1}(s,0)^Tv}{|F_{j-1}(s,0)^Tv|}.
\]
Round $(\widehat k_j/\ell_j)m_0^*$ to a nearest integer vector
$\nu_j\in\mathbb Z^2$, and define
\begin{equation}
 k_j=\ell_j|\nu_j|,\qquad m_{0,j}=\nu_j/|\nu_j|.
 \label{ipm:cascade-rounding}
\end{equation}
Then $|k_j-\widehat k_j|\le C\ell_j$ and
$|m_{0,j}-m_0^*|\le C\ell_j/\widehat k_j$.
To check the loss under $F^{-T}$, use
$|v/|v|-w/|w||\le2|v-w|/|w|$ for nonzero $v,w$,
with $v=F^{-T}m_{0,j}$ and $w=F^{-T}m_0^*$.
The denominator is at least $\|F\|^{-1}$.
The resulting unit-vector difference is at most
$2\|F^{-1}\|\|F\||m_{0,j}-m_0^*|$.
For differences below $1/2$, the angular difference is at most
twice the unit-vector difference. Both matrix norms have fixed
polynomial bounds in $K_{j-1}$, proving
\begin{equation}
 |\tau_j|\le CK_{j-1}^A\ell_j/\widehat k_j,\qquad
 |\tau_j|j^2x_{j-1}\ll1.
 \label{ipm:cascade-rounding-error}
\end{equation}
The logarithm of the right side in the second assertion is bounded
above by
$C+\log(j^2x_{j-1})+A\log K_{j-1}
 -x_{j-1}/j^{7/2}-x_{j-1}/j^2$.
It tends to a large negative value uniformly with the base by
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-rounded-logs} and
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-polynomial-uniformity}.
Thus it satisfies the fixed transfer tolerance.
This establishes the exact periodicity condition
$(k_j/\ell_j)m_{0,j}\in\mathbb Z^2$ with an admissible angular error.

The parent is already known, so Lemma~\ref{ipm:amplification} now
determines a first target time $t_j$ satisfying \eqref{ipm:transfer-time}: 
\begin{equation}
 \beta_j(t_j)=\pi/2+1/x_j,\qquad
 W_j/4\le t_j-t_{j-1}\le3W_j/4.
 \label{ipm:cascade-target-time}
\end{equation}
Fix the new horizon
\begin{equation}
 S_j=t_j+2W_{j+1}.
 \label{ipm:cascade-new-horizon}
\end{equation}
By \eqref{ipm:cascade-scale-horizons},
$S_j\le t_{j-1}+(3/4+1/8)W_j<S_{j-1}$. Normalize the seed by
\eqref{ipm:cascade-target-normalization}, with target $h_j$, and choose
$\Gamma_j$ by \eqref{ipm:cascade-gamma-choice}.
The inherited gradient bound supplies the future estimates required
for that choice. All the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{ipm:packet} now
hold, by Lemma~\ref{ipm:scales}. Apply it on the already fixed interval
$[0,S_j]$ to obtain $P_j$.

In particular the terminal condition used to select the layer's phase
means is imposed only after $S_j$ is known. Neither $t_j$ nor $S_j$
depends on the child mean being solved: both use only the existing
parent and the prescribed next width. This fixes the dependency order
without a shooting or fixed-point problem.

Combining the leading pressure-Hessian bound \eqref{ipm:cascade-h-budget} with the
pressure-Hessian remainder in \eqref{ipm:packet-gradient-errors} gives
\[
 H(P_j)\le H(P_{j-1})+
 \bigl[C_H(\delta_jh_j+h_je^{-c_0x_{j-1}})+\epsilon_j\bigr]I
 \le b_jI\le(3/4)I<I,
\]
by \eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-summed-budget}.
The global gradient bound $\|\nabla P_j\|_\infty\le C_{\nabla}h_j$
follows from \eqref{ipm:seventh-cascade-gradient-induction}.
The layer output estimate \eqref{ipm:packet-output-class}, with
$C_{\rm out}\ge C_*$, and the second inequality in
\eqref{ipm:cascade-scale-output} give
\[
 K_j\le(K_{j-1}\mathcal P_jk_j/\ell_j)^{C_{\rm out}}
     \le k_j^{A_K}.
\]
The prescribed leading normalization \eqref{ipm:cascade-target-normalization}
and target angle at the origin \eqref{ipm:cascade-target-time} also hold,
so the induction is complete.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{ipm:common-time}
The times from Proposition~\ref{ipm:stage-induction} have a limit
$T\in(0,1)$ and satisfy
\[
 0<T-t_j\le\tfrac45W_{j+1}<2W_{j+1},\qquad T<S_j.
\]
Every finite stage is therefore smooth on a neighborhood of $[0,T]$.
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
The target times are strictly increasing and their increments are
comparable to $W_j$. Since $W_{j+1}\le W_j/16$, their limit
\[
 T=\lim_{j\to\infty}t_j
\]
is finite and positive. More precisely,
\[
 0<T-t_j\le\frac34\sum_{i>j}W_i
      \le\frac{3/4}{1-1/16}W_{j+1}=\frac45W_{j+1}<2W_{j+1},\qquad T<S_j\le1.
\]
The final inequality is strict. Thus every finite stage is smooth on a
neighborhood of $[0,T]$.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- proposition-14: body_md -->
\label{ipm:initial-data-convergence}
For every fixed integer $m\ge0$, the increments
$\theta_j=P_j-P_{j-1}$ satisfy
\[
 \sum_{j\ge J}\sup_{0\le t\le t_{j-1}}
                   \|\theta_j(t)\|_{C^m}<\infty.
\]
Consequently $P_j(0)$ converges in $C^\infty$ to a single odd mean-zero
density $\rho_{\rm in}$. The stage sequence and its initial datum are
independent of the derivative order $m$.
<!-- end proposition-14 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Set $\theta_j=P_j-P_{j-1}$. For $t\le t_{j-1}$,
\eqref{ipm:cascade-gamma-history} and
\eqref{ipm:cascade-osc-bound} give, at each fixed derivative order $m$,
\begin{align}
 \|\theta_{j,\rm osc}(t)\|_{C^m}
    &\le C_m\ell_j^{1-m}k_j^{m-1}\mathcal P_j^{C_m}
                     h_j e^{-c_0x_{j-1}}
       \le C_m e^{-c_0x_{j-1}/2},
       \label{ipm:cascade-history-cm-osc}\\
 \|\theta_{j,\rm mean}(t)\|_{C^m}
    &\le C_m\ell_j^{1-m}k_j^{-2}\mathcal P_j^{C_m}
       \le C_m e^{-x_{j-1}/j^2},
       \label{ipm:cascade-history-cm-mean}
\end{align}
for all sufficiently large $j$ depending on $m$. We have absorbed the
fixed power of $K$ and the factor $\delta_j\le1$ into the displayed
$\mathcal P_j$ power. To verify these inequalities, take logarithms:
apart from $\log C_m$, their respective upper bounds are
\[
 -c_0x_{j-1}+\frac{2m x_{j-1}}{j^2}
                   +\frac{D_mx_{j-1}}{j^3},\qquad
 -\frac{9x_{j-1}}{5j^2}+\frac{D_mx_{j-1}}{j^3}.
\]
Here $D_m$ is finite, independent of the stage, and may depend on
$m$ and the already fixed constants. These bounds follow by inserting
the rounded carrier inequalities, $\log\ell_j^{-1}=x_{j-1}/j^{7/2}$,
$\log\mathcal P_j\le D x_{j-1}/j^3$, and
$\log h_j=x_{j-1}/j^5$.
For $m=0$ any negative carrier or localization exponents can be
dropped when forming an upper bound.
Choose a tail index $j_m\ge J$ such that
$2m/j_m^2\le c_0/4$,
$D_m/j_m^3\le c_0/4$, and $D_m/j_m\le1/2$.
The same inequalities then hold for all $j\ge j_m$ and imply
\eqref{ipm:cascade-history-cm-osc} and
\eqref{ipm:cascade-history-cm-mean}.

For the exact correction, its logarithmic prefactor is bounded by
$D'_m(\log k_j+\log\ell_j^{-1}+\log\mathcal P_j)$.
This grows only linearly in $\log k_j$ for fixed $m$, while
$k_j^\vartheta=\exp(\vartheta\log k_j)$.
Increase $j_m$, if necessary, so that this prefactor is at most
$k_j^\vartheta/4$. The correction is then bounded by
$C_m\exp(-k_j^\vartheta/4)$, whose sum converges. For example
$k_j^\vartheta/4\ge x_{j-1}/j^2$ eventually, by the lower carrier
bound and elementary exponential growth, and
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-doubling} applies.
The two other right sides in
\eqref{ipm:cascade-history-cm-osc}--\eqref{ipm:cascade-history-cm-mean}
are summable by the same doubling argument. The finitely many terms
with $J\le j<j_m$ are smooth on their respective compact intervals.
Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j\ge J}\sup_{0\le t\le t_{j-1}}
                      \|\theta_j(t)\|_{C^m}<\infty
 \qquad\text{for every fixed }m.
 \label{ipm:cascade-full-history-convergence}
\end{equation}
In particular the initial data converge in $C^\infty$ to an odd
mean-zero function $\rho_{\rm in}$. This uses the actual small seed
$\gamma_j(0)$; a bound by the maximum amplitude up to the target would
not suffice.
The constants $C_m,D_m,j_m$ are chosen only in this convergence
argument, after the entire sequence has been constructed. Thus
convergence in all fixed $C^m$ norms is asserted for a single
initial datum. No scale or base choice depends on infinitely many
derivative-order constants.
\label{ipm:expanded-cascade-fixed-order-tails}
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- proposition-15: body_md -->
\label{ipm:smooth-convergence}
On every compact subinterval of $[0,T)$, the stages converge with all
mixed space-time derivatives to an exact unforced solution
$\rho\in C^\infty([0,T)\times\mathbb T^2)$ with
$\rho(0)=\rho_{\rm in}$, where $\rho_{\rm in}$ is the datum of
Proposition~\ref{ipm:initial-data-convergence}.
<!-- end proposition-15 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Fix $t_0<T$. Eventually $t_{j-1}>t_0$, so
\eqref{ipm:cascade-full-history-convergence} shows that $P_j$ converges
on $[0,t_0]$ in every spatial $C^m$ norm. Denote the limit by $\rho$.
Here is the passage through time derivatives in fixed Banach spaces.
For every integer $s\ge2$, spatial convergence gives
$P_j\to\rho$ in $C([0,t_0];H^s)$.
The bounded Fourier symbol gives
$\|u(f)\|_{H^s}\le c\|f\|_{H^s}$.
Since $H^s(\mathbb T^2)$ is an algebra for $s\ge2$,
the bilinear map
\[
 (f,g)\longmapsto u(f)\cdot\nabla g
\]
is continuous from $H^{s+1}\times H^{s+1}$ to $H^s$.
Therefore the exact stage equations imply
$\partial_tP_j\to-u(\rho)\cdot\nabla\rho$
in $C([0,t_0];H^s)$ for every such $s$.
Passing to the integral equation
$P_j(t)=P_j(0)+\int_0^t\partial_tP_j(\sigma)\,d\sigma$
proves that $\rho$ has this derivative and solves
\eqref{ipm:darcy}--\eqref{ipm:unforced}.

For the induction step, suppose convergence of
$\partial_t^aP_j$ has been proved in
$C([0,t_0];H^s)$ for all $a\le q$ and every fixed $s\ge2$.
Differentiate the classical stage equation $q$ times:
\begin{equation}
 \partial_t^{q+1}P_j
  =-\sum_{a=0}^q\binom qa
      u(\partial_t^aP_j)\cdot\nabla\partial_t^{q-a}P_j.
 \label{ipm:expanded-cascade-time-recursion}
\end{equation}
The induction hypothesis at $s+1$ and the bilinear continuity just
proved make the right side converge in $C([0,t_0];H^s)$.
The fundamental theorem of calculus applied to
$\partial_t^qP_j$ then shows that its limit is differentiable in
$H^s$ with this limiting derivative. This proves the induction
for $q+1$, simultaneously for every fixed $s$.
The velocity commutes with time differentiation because it is a
bounded linear map on each of these spaces.
Finally Sobolev embedding at arbitrarily large $s$ gives convergence
and continuity of every mixed space-time derivative.
Thus
\begin{equation}
 \rho\in C^\infty([0,T)\times\mathbb T^2),\qquad
 \rho(0)=\rho_{\rm in},
 \label{ipm:cascade-smooth-limit}
\end{equation}
and $\rho$ is an exact odd mean-zero unforced solution on the whole
interval $[0,T)$. No stability assertion across the accumulation time
is required.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- proposition-16: body_md -->
\label{ipm:component-divergence}
The solution of Proposition~\ref{ipm:smooth-convergence} satisfies
\[
 \lim_{t\uparrow T}\partial_2\rho(t,0)
 =\lim_{t\uparrow T}\partial_1u_1(\rho)(t,0)=+\infty.
\]
More precisely, there are fixed $a_1,a_2>0$ and a sequence
$D_j\to0$ such that, for every $t\in[t_j,T)$,
\begin{equation}
 \partial_2\rho(t,0)\ge a_1h_j-2/c-D_j,\qquad
 \partial_1u_1(\rho)(t,0)\ge a_2h_j/x_j^2-1-D_j.
 \label{ipm:cascade-limit-lower}
\end{equation}
In particular, the solution has no
smooth continuation through $T$.
<!-- end proposition-16 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
Let $g_j$ and $\beta_j$ denote the magnitude and angle of the leading
density-gradient increment of layer $j$ at the origin. On its active and retained interval
$[t_{j-1},S_j]$ its phase has positive second component. Its two useful
leading contributions are
\begin{equation}
 (\partial_2\theta_j)_{\rm lead}=g_j\sin\beta_j\ge0,\qquad
 (\partial_1u_1(\theta_j))_{\rm lead}
          =c g_j\cos^2\beta_j\sin\beta_j\ge0.
 \label{ipm:cascade-positive-components}
\end{equation}
The square of the first phase component in the second formula is
essential. These signs hold even while a layer crosses the vertical
direction.
To derive the second component without an orientation convention,
the leading velocity-gradient increment is
$c g_j((n_j)_1(n_j)_2,-(n_j)_1^2)\otimes n_j$ by
\eqref{ipm:velocity-symbol}; its $(1,1)$ entry is exactly
$c g_j(n_j)_1^2(n_j)_2$.
Oddness of the parent flow sends label zero to spatial zero, so the
phase in these formulas is exactly zero at the observation point.
The factor $f'_{\delta_j}(0)/\delta_j^{-1}$ is one, and no
oscillatory sign has been discarded.

At the target $g_j(t_j)=h_j$. By
\eqref{ipm:rayidentity} and $H(P_{j-1})\le I$,
\begin{equation}
 g_j(t)\ge e^{-(t-t_j)}h_j\ge e^{-1}h_j,\qquad
 t_j\le t\le S_j.
 \label{ipm:cascade-persistent-amplitude}
\end{equation}
The parent gradient bound and the matrix bounds \eqref{ipm:matrixbounds} yield
\[
 |\beta_j(t)-\beta_j(t_j)|
       \le C(1+h_{j-1})W_{j+1}\le\frac1{4x_j}.
\]
Thus, throughout $[t_j,S_j]$, its angle lies between
$\pi/2+3/(4x_j)$ and $\pi/2+5/(4x_j)$. For the chosen large base,
there are fixed $a_1,a_2>0$ such that
\begin{equation}
 (\partial_2\theta_j)_{\rm lead}\ge a_1h_j,\qquad
 (\partial_1u_1(\theta_j))_{\rm lead}\ge a_2h_j/x_j^2.
 \label{ipm:cascade-persistent-components}
\end{equation}
For example $\sin\beta_j\ge1/2$ on this interval.
Since $|\cos\beta_j|=\sin(\beta_j-\pi/2)$ and
$0<\beta_j-\pi/2\le1$ for the chosen base,
$\sin v\ge v/2$ gives $|\cos\beta_j|\ge3/(8x_j)$.
Together with $g_j\ge e^{-1}h_j$, these yield the displayed
bounds with $a_1=1/(2e)$ and $a_2=9c/(128e)$, or with
any smaller fixed constants.

For times before activation, \eqref{ipm:cascade-small-history} and the
global expansions instead give
\begin{equation}
 \|\nabla\theta_j(t)\|_\infty
       +\|\nabla u(\theta_j)(t)\|_\infty
       \le d_j+C\epsilon_j,\qquad
 d_j=Ch_je^{-c_0x_{j-1}},\qquad t\le t_{j-1}.
 \label{ipm:cascade-preactivation-error}
\end{equation}
Both $\sum d_j$ and $\sum\epsilon_j$ converge. Define
$D_j=C\sum_{i\ge j}(d_i+\epsilon_i)$, so $D_j\to0$.

Every earlier exact parent satisfies the pointwise bounds
\begin{equation}
 \partial_2P_{j-1}=-\operatorname{tr}H(P_{j-1})/c\ge-2/c,\qquad
 \partial_1u_1(P_{j-1})=-H_{11}(P_{j-1})\ge-1.
 \label{ipm:cascade-parent-lower}
\end{equation}
Fix $j$ and any $t\in[t_j,T)$. Telescope from $P_{j-1}$ through finitely
many later stages. Layer $j$ has the lower bounds
\eqref{ipm:cascade-persistent-components}; every later active layer
has nonnegative leading contributions by
\eqref{ipm:cascade-positive-components}; every later layer in history
costs at most \eqref{ipm:cascade-preactivation-error}. Sum the global
remainder bounds and then pass to the smooth limit at this fixed time.
In detail, for every finite $n\ge j$, $P_n(t)$ and every increment
in $P_n=P_{j-1}+\sum_{i=j}^n\theta_i$ are defined because
$t<T<S_n$ and the horizons decrease. If $i>j$ and $t\ge t_{i-1}$,
the leading contribution is nonnegative and its error is at most
$\epsilon_i$. If $t<t_{i-1}$, its entire contribution has lower
bound $-d_i-C\epsilon_i$. These two alternatives exhaust all later
layers. The sum of their lower errors is bounded by $D_j$,
independently of both $n$ and $t$. At the fixed time $t<T$, the
smooth convergence already proved allows $n\to\infty$ in these
two derivative components, including the velocity component.
This proves \eqref{ipm:cascade-limit-lower} uniformly for all
$t\in[t_j,T)$.
Both $h_j$ and $h_j/x_j^2$ tend to infinity, since
$\log h_j=x_{j-1}/j^5$ dominates every fixed multiple of $\log x_j$.
For the latter assertion, insert $x_j=j^2x_{j-1}$ and apply
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-polynomial-uniformity}, or its
fixed-base asymptotic as $j\to\infty$, to
$(2\log x_{j-1}+4\log j)/(x_{j-1}/j^5)$.
The ratio tends to zero because of the factorial growth.
