Given any finite lower threshold, choose $j$ large in
\eqref{ipm:cascade-limit-lower}. The threshold then holds at every
subsequent time below $T$, not merely at the target times. Therefore
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{t\uparrow T}\partial_2\rho(t,0)
   =\lim_{t\uparrow T}\partial_1u_1(\rho)(t,0)=+\infty.
 \label{ipm:cascade-component-limits}
\end{equation}
In particular both $L^\infty$ gradient norms tend to infinity. Combined
with \eqref{ipm:cascade-smooth-limit}, this excludes any smooth
continuation through $T$.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{ipm:seventh-physical-uniqueness}
Two smooth solutions of the normalized unforced IPM equation on
$[0,\tau]\times\mathbb T^2$ with identical initial density agree on that
interval. More quantitatively, if they are $\rho,\sigma$ and
$w=\rho-\sigma$, then
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:seventh-physical-stability}
 \|w(t)\|_2^2
 \le\|w(0)\|_2^2
 \exp\left(2c\int_0^t\|\nabla\sigma(s)\|_\infty\,ds\right).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
Linearity of the Darcy map gives the exact difference equation
\[
 w_t+u(\rho)\cdot\nabla w+u(w)\cdot\nabla\sigma=0.
\]
Pair with $w$ in $L^2$. The first transport term integrates to zero by
periodicity and $\operatorname{div}u(\rho)=0$. Thus
\[
 \frac12\frac d{dt}\|w\|_2^2
 =-\int_{\mathbb T^2}w\,u(w)\cdot\nabla\sigma
 \le c\|\nabla\sigma\|_\infty\|w\|_2^2
\]
because the Darcy symbol in \eqref{ipm:velocity-symbol} satisfies
$|\mathfrak m|\le c$. Integrating this scalar differential
inequality proves \eqref{ipm:seventh-physical-stability}. On a compact
smooth interval its exponent is finite. Zero initial difference
therefore implies zero difference throughout.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
This also verifies precisely the initial-data issue in removing a
force. If a correction erased the entire perturbation residual while
preserving the parent's initial density, its exact smooth output would
equal the parent by the lemma. Our initial density includes the primary
oscillatory seed and the selected phase means before the zero-initial
residual correction is applied. That distinction is an exact
initial-data distinction; no failure of uniqueness is being used.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{ipm:maximal-lifespan}
The solution of Proposition~\ref{ipm:smooth-convergence} is the
unique smooth solution with initial density $\rho_{\rm in}$ on
every compact subinterval of $[0,T)$. Its maximal smooth lifespan
is $T$.
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
The smooth limit in \eqref{ipm:cascade-smooth-limit} is consequently the
unique smooth solution with its limiting initial density on every
compact subinterval of $[0,T)$. Its maximal smooth lifespan is $T$:
the construction supplies existence up to every smaller time, whereas
any smooth extension to a time greater than $T$ would coincide with it
below $T$ and have bounded first derivatives near $T$, contradicting
\eqref{ipm:cascade-component-limits}. This last argument uses the
proved component limits, without an additional continuation criterion.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- proposition-17: body_md -->
\label{ipm:terminal-holder}
For each $0\le\eta<1$, the stage sequence converges in
$C([0,T];C^\eta(\mathbb T^2))$. These limits agree with the
smooth solution on $[0,T)$ and have one common terminal value
$\rho_*\in\bigcap_{0\le\eta<1}C^\eta(\mathbb T^2)$.
The terminal value is odd and mean zero, and
$\rho(t)\to\rho_*$ in every such norm as $t\uparrow T$.
<!-- end proposition-17 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
The amplitude choice \eqref{ipm:cascade-gamma-choice} and the all-time
layer bounds give, uniformly for $0\le t\le T$,
\begin{equation}
 \|\theta_j(t)\|_{C^0}\le C\ell_jk_j^{-1}\mathcal P_j^C,\qquad
 \|\theta_j(t)\|_{C^1}\le\mathcal P_j^C.
 \label{ipm:cascade-alltime-low}
\end{equation}
Here the exact correction is smaller than the displayed quantities for
large $j$; finitely many initial stages cause no problem. For the second
bound one can either use the layer estimate with $m=1$, or the global
gradient estimate and
$h_j=\gamma_j(t_j)|m_j(t_j,0)|/\delta_j\le\mathcal P_j^C$.
For the first bound, the oscillatory term has $m=0$ in
\eqref{ipm:cascade-osc-bound} and $\gamma_j\le\Gamma_j\le\mathcal P_j$.
The mean term is bounded by
$C\ell_jk_j^{-2}\mathcal P_j^C\le
C\ell_jk_j^{-1}\mathcal P_j^C$.
Even using the weakened correction bound, its ratio to
$\ell_jk_j^{-1}\mathcal P_j^C$ is at most a fixed polynomial in
$k_j/\ell_j,\mathcal P_j$ times $e^{-k_j^\vartheta/2}$, which
tends to zero. For $m=1$, the same reasoning gives the second
bound. Thus only the fixed constants for derivative orders zero
and one occur in \eqref{ipm:cascade-alltime-low}; their values do
not have to be uniform over all derivative orders.

For $0<\eta<1$, the elementary interpolation inequality
\begin{equation}
 \|f\|_{C^\eta}
      \le C_\eta\|f\|_{C^0}^{1-\eta}\|f\|_{C^1}^{\eta}
 \label{ipm:cascade-interpolation}
\end{equation}
follows by bounding a difference quotient with the minimum of
$2\|f\|_\infty$ and $\|\nabla f\|_\infty|x-y|$, and splitting at
$|x-y|=2\|f\|_\infty/\|\nabla f\|_\infty$.
If the gradient norm is zero, the function is constant and the
inequality is immediate, with the full $C^1$ norm understood to
include the supremum norm. The same supremum term bounds the
zeroth-order part of the $C^\eta$ norm.
Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{0\le t\le T}\|\theta_j(t)\|_{C^\eta}
       \le C_\eta k_j^{-(1-\eta)}\mathcal P_j^{C_\eta}
       \le C_\eta k_j^{-(1-\eta)/2}
 \label{ipm:cascade-holder-sum}
\end{equation}
for all sufficiently large $j$ depending on $\eta$. We used
$\ell_j^{1-\eta}\le1$ and
$\log\mathcal P_j/\log k_j\to0$. The right side is summable.
Explicitly, since $\log\mathcal P_j/\log k_j\le2D/j$, take
$j_\eta\ge4DC_\eta/(1-\eta)$, after including any fixed
prefactor in the harmless outside constant. Then the exponent
from $\mathcal P_j^{C_\eta}$ is at most
$(1-\eta)\log k_j/2$. For this fixed $\eta$,
\eqref{ipm:expanded-cascade-doubling} with $q=2$ gives summability
of the remaining carrier powers. The choice of $j_\eta$ does not
alter the construction when $\eta$ approaches one.
The case $\eta=0$ follows directly from
\eqref{ipm:cascade-alltime-low}.

Each finite stage is smooth past $T$ by
Lemma~\ref{ipm:common-time}. The telescoping density series therefore
converges in $C([0,T];C^\eta)$ for every $0\le\eta<1$. Its restriction
to $[0,T)$ is the previously constructed smooth solution. In particular
there is a terminal density, common to all these spaces, such that
\begin{equation}
 \rho_*\in\bigcap_{0\le\eta<1}C^\eta(\mathbb T^2),\qquad
 \rho(t)\longrightarrow\rho_*\quad\hbox{in }C^\eta
                         \quad(t\uparrow T).
 \label{ipm:cascade-terminal-density}
\end{equation}
The terminal densities agree because their $C^0$ limits agree. Oddness
and zero mean pass to this limit.
For completeness, the space $C^\eta(\mathbb T^2)$ is Banach in the
usual supremum-plus-H\"older-seminorm norm. A series whose suprema
of these norms are summable is uniformly Cauchy in
$C([0,T];C^\eta)$, and its uniform limit is continuous. This proves
the continuity assertion at $T$ directly. It requires neither a
uniform $C^1$ bound on the total solution nor an equation for a
smooth extension past $T$; the component limits rule out such an
extension.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{ipm:cascade}
The finite-stage construction of Proposition~\ref{ipm:packet} yields an odd,
mean-zero initial density $\rho_{\rm in}\in C^\infty(\mathbb T^2)$ and an
exact unforced IPM solution smooth on $[0,1)\times\mathbb T^2$ such that
\[
 \lim_{t\uparrow1}\partial_2\rho(t,0)
   =\lim_{t\uparrow1}\partial_1u_1(\rho)(t,0)=+\infty.
\]
It has a terminal density in every $C^\eta$, $0\le\eta<1$, with
convergence in each such norm.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
Proposition~\ref{ipm:stage-induction} constructs the stages, and
Lemma~\ref{ipm:common-time} supplies their accumulation time.
Propositions~\ref{ipm:smooth-convergence},
\ref{ipm:component-divergence}, and \ref{ipm:terminal-holder}
give the asserted solution and limits with terminal time $T>0$.
Define $\widetilde\rho(t,x)=T\rho(Tt,x)$. Since the Darcy velocity is
linear in its density,
\[
 \partial_t\widetilde\rho
       +u(\widetilde\rho)\cdot\nabla\widetilde\rho
       =T^2\bigl(\partial_t\rho+u(\rho)\cdot\nabla\rho\bigr)(Tt,x)=0.
\]
This moves the singular time to one. Multiplication by the positive
constant $T$ preserves both component limits, smoothness of the initial
density, oddness, zero mean, and every stated terminal convergence.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{ipm:seventh-foundations-sobolev}
Let
$\mathbb T_L^d=\prod_{i=1}^d\mathbb R/(L_i\mathbb Z)$, with
$L_i\ge L_0>0$, and use unnormalized Lebesgue measure.
For fixed $s>d/2$ the $H^s$ algebra constant is bounded independently
of the $L_i$. For $s>d/2+1$ the same is true of the embedding
$H^s\longrightarrow W^{1,\infty}$.
For an integer $s\ge0$, multiplication from
$W^{s,\infty}\times H^s$ to $H^s$ also has a uniform constant.
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
Put $V=\prod_iL_i$, and normalize Fourier coefficients by $V^{-1}$.
The frequency lattice is
$\Xi_L=\prod_i(2\pi/L_i)\mathbb Z$, and
\[
 \|f\|_{H^s}^2
   =V\sum_{\xi\in\Xi_L}\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}|\widehat f(\xi)|^2.
\]
For every $a>d/2$,
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:seventh-foundations-lattice-sum}
 V^{-1}\sum_{\xi\in\Xi_L}\langle\xi\rangle^{-2a}
       \le C_{a,d,L_0}.
\end{equation}
Indeed the rectangular cell centered at $\xi$ has volume
$(2\pi)^d/V$, and its diameter is bounded by a number depending only
on $d,L_0$. Throughout that cell $\langle x\rangle$ and
$\langle\xi\rangle$ are comparable with a uniform constant.
Multiply the sum by its cell volume and compare it with
$\int_{\mathbb R^d}\langle x\rangle^{-2a}\,dx$.

Cauchy--Schwarz and
\eqref{ipm:seventh-foundations-lattice-sum} give
\[
 \sum_\xi|\widehat f(\xi)|\le C_{s,d,L_0}^{1/2}\|f\|_{H^s}
 \quad(s>d/2).
\]
Applying the same calculation to the first derivatives gives the
$W^{1,\infty}$ estimate when $s-1>d/2$.
For the algebra estimate use $\langle\xi+\zeta\rangle
\le\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\zeta\rangle$ and
\[
 \langle\xi+\zeta\rangle^s
   \le2^{\max(s-1,0)}(\langle\xi\rangle^s+\langle\zeta\rangle^s)
\]
(the factor is $2^{s-1}$ for $s\ge1$)
in the convolution formula for $\widehat{fg}$ and then the
$\ell^2*\ell^1\to\ell^2$ inequality on the counting lattice.
Multiplying by $V^{1/2}$ and using the preceding Fourier
$\ell^1$ bound yields
$\|fg\|_{H^s}\le C_s\|f\|_{H^s}\|g\|_{H^s}$.
All factors of $V$ cancel. Finally, for the multiplier estimate,
expand only the fixed number $s$ of derivatives by Leibniz's rule,
put the coefficient derivatives in $L^\infty$, and put the function
derivatives in $L^2$. Equivalence between this derivative norm and
the displayed Fourier norm depends only on $s,d$.
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{ipm:time-projected-inverse}
Let $\Pi$ be a fixed orthogonal projection on
$L^2(\mathbb T_L^d;\mathbb R^r)$ commuting with translations, and set
$\mathcal H=\operatorname{ran}\Pi$. Fix an integer $s\ge0$.
Let $A(t,x)$ be a smooth symmetric matrix field in $x$, continuous
in $t$ with values in $W^{s,\infty}$, and suppose
$A(t,x)\ge\lambda I$ for a fixed $\lambda>0$.
Define $T_{A(t)}v=\Pi(A(t)v)$ on $\mathcal H$.
Then $T_{A(t)}$ is invertible on $\mathcal H\cap H^s$, and
$t\mapsto T_{A(t)}^{-1}$ is continuous in operator norm there.
If $A\in C_t^1W^{s,\infty}$ and $h\in C_t^1H^s$, then
$v=T_A^{-1}\Pi h\in C_t^1H^s$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{ipm:seventh-foundations-time-inverse}
 v_t=T_A^{-1}\Pi(h_t-A_tv).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
Coercivity and self-adjointness give an $L^2$ inverse of norm at
most $\lambda^{-1}$. Commuting spatial difference quotients with
$\Pi$, as in the proof of Proposition~\ref{ipm:meaninverse}, gives
bounded inversion on $\mathcal H\cap H^s$, locally uniformly in time.
The resolvent identity
\[
 T_{A(t)}^{-1}-T_{A(u)}^{-1}
   =T_{A(t)}^{-1}\Pi(A(u)-A(t))T_{A(u)}^{-1}
\]
holds on the projected $H^s$ space and proves operator continuity.
For differentiable $A,h$, taking a difference quotient gives
\eqref{ipm:seventh-foundations-time-inverse}.
<!-- end proof-41 -->
